mr president, the single market is europe's greatest achievement, a true multiplier of freedom and possibilities for us all. however, in times of crisis, the latter is too often the victim of national reflexes and egos. what's the proof? in the face of the energy crisis, instead of pooling our efforts and creating a european sovereign fund which would give us greater strategic autonomy and protect all our citizens, some are arguing only in favour of national aid. but national-only responses risk exacerbating divisions and fragmentation of the market. it is a complete illusion to think that each for himself will be able to alleviate the gigantic crises that await us. in 2022, the cost of national barriers in our market was 300 billion, a loss of 2% of gdp. in 1988, mr cecchini wrote a report on the cost of non-marketing; i think we should write a new report on the cost of not having a single market. we must also be more efficient in applying market rules by taking a lead from competition policy. this is another good reason to amend the treaties and reform our union.
mr president, madam minister, welcome, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, the single market is and remains an achievement to be defended. but now we must put the real economy and small and mediumsized enterprises back at the centre and not speculative finance and large multinationals. after brexit, covid and ukraine, it is becoming increasingly clear that we need to shorten value chains; bring strategic production back to europe; protect the quality of our goods; demand from third countries, starting with china, the same standards as ours; standardise customs procedures; reduce taxes; reduce bureaucracy and hyper-regulation and invest in skills. and in the face of anti-inflation plans such as the american one, we must imagine common instruments and not responses that benefit only a few, destroying the internal market. only then can it still be an added value and not lead to new inequalities and unfair competition.
mr president we celebrate, but we know very well that the single market is still not working perfectly. the benefits are unevenly distributed, with some member states and their companies benefiting more than others. one study estimates that the current relaxation of state aid rules will benefit french and german companies by 80 percent. the commission should guarantee that everyone has a fair chance in the single market. this principle has been violated by the mobility package and the regulation of posted workers, and now with regard to subsidies. furthermore, by withholding eu funds from hungary and poland, the commission is putting those countries at a disadvantage in the single market, and by blocking erasmus, it is also putting young people at a disadvantage. it is doing all this at a time when war and sanctions have sent energy prices soaring, inflation soaring at an inhumane rate and the machinations of our competitors - see the us green protectionist policy - are already weakening europe's competitiveness. i believe that this is contrary to the commission's mandate. please, madam commissioner, do what you can to put an end to this illegal situation and to allow hungarians and poles to compete on equal terms in the single market.
president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, i am a child of the 1990s, raised with the single market as we know it today: the european union is a global economy with almost 500 million consumers, immense mobility between european member states and no more border controls. my region, west flanders in belgium, has grown thanks to the european single market. suddenly, it became possible to trade goods in the neighbouring countries and elsewhere in the eu without many obstacles. the trade in agri-food products in particular has not been a winning strategy for us. it is not for nothing that we are today called the vegetable garden of europe. that is thanks to the single market. we are immensely proud of that. many people know the single market best from the food on our plates. colleagues, we cannot rest on our laurels now. it is our duty to keep the single market as healthy as possible in the future. i am convinced that it is and will remain the glue of our cooperation as europeans. and that's what our approach to every crisis is. for example, with the rise of big tech, we're deepening our digital single market, since the pandemic, our health union, and now our energy and our defense union are top of mind. so let us, on this 30th anniversary, be more aware than ever that the internal market is the foundation of our peaceful european cooperation, but above all it is also a boost to the creation of wealth and a lever to strengthen the union's position in the world.
mr president, madam minister, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, the internal market - everyone has said this here - is the heart of the european union, it is the platform. you called her all sorts of things. yeah, it's like a house: the base, the foundation. but it's not just the foundation that's important. it's how we build. that is why i think it is good to think 30 years from now: how to modernise the internal market, how to make it strong in global competition, how can we make those rights, enshrined in the treaty: the free movement of workers and the free movement of goods, exist? i'm not going to use the term single market that you used because, from my point of view, as a romanian, it's not a single market, it's a domestic market, but it's not a single market. so i hope that sweden has the ambition to merge the two now fragmented segments: the schengen and non-schengen market. and i also think, commissioner, that it is very important how we defend, how we protect the internal market. i therefore believe that the commission should present a report on the implementation of the unfair practices directive, because we know very well that we still have problems with imports from third countries which create unfair competition for producers. and then, and a safety for consumer protection. i believe that we all have an obligation: the member states, the commission, the council, parliament and, ultimately, the citizens, to protect the internal market, because it is good for everyone.
mr president, madam executive vice-president, madam minister, the european single market is undoubtedly one of the greatest projects and successes of the european union. a market of some 450 million consumers applying harmonised rules in almost all areas is the greatest asset of us european citizens. free movement makes the exchange of goods much easier, but we must move on to finalising this great project, which is not complete: without free movement of people, for the trains that carry these goods, which are at the internal borders. the waves of immigrants, then the covid-19 pandemic, have caused many states to reinstate controls, and even after the real danger has passed, free movement has been much harder to return to normal. even so, the fact that not all eu countries are included in the schengen area, as is the case in my country, romania, creates great difficulties for the economy and producers of the countries which, although they are members of the union, are still held at the border of the european union, but also for those who transport goods in these countries. a free movement of goods cannot exist without the same freedom of citizens. for the european single market to be a real success, we need all member states to be a real part of the european union. if we want a single market, we must remove these last obstacles.
mr. president, pope francis said that we live in a throwaway culture, and he meant the sick, the elderly, and the unwanted in society. we also face a culture - or not - of excessive waste generation and accumulation that pollutes and burdens our planet. we even trade in waste, so we are talking about the internal market for waste shipments. often, there is a great deal of money involved in waste, especially if it is disposed of improperly and illegally. i therefore believe that this area of trade and shipment of waste out of the place of origin should be regulated in depth and that its implementation should be monitored correctly and effectively. but we can no longer allow waste to be a source of quick profit and wealth at the expense of polluting our planet.
mr president, ladies and gentlemen, the revision of the waste shipment regulation is important legislation to protect both the environment and human health, but at the same time it has enormous potential to accelerate the union's transition to the circular economy. the transport of waste is an activity which requires clear rules and adequate controls. unfortunately, even today, between 15 and 30% of shipments are made illegally. this is a completely unacceptable situation which this regulation seeks to remedy. we remember well how china's decision to no longer accept plastic waste has had a strong impact on our ability to manage it properly. the text adopted by the envi committee, which provides for a gradual limitation of their export, wants this not to happen again. instead, the aim is to create a steering wheel both for designing more sustainable products and for further developing our recycling capacity. the export of our waste to the third world can no longer be the solution, and it must not be, but we must move towards the circular economy without delay and this regulation is a decisive step forward.
mr president, commissioner, as you are well aware, illegal shipments of waste have reached alarming levels within the european union and are having serious and long-term consequences for public health and the environment. this situation needs to be corrected urgently and i hereby express my firm support for the ambitious agreement reached in the envi committee on the revision of the legislation on waste shipments, which includes clear and strict rules to combat illegal waste traffic. i also support the central role that the european anti-fraud office should play in transnational investigations. strengthening cooperation between member states, tightening the sanctions regime to deter criminal activities in this area and finally draw attention to the urgent need to create the european action plan to combat illegal shipments of waste, in order to ensure our success in the fight against this phenomenon.
mr president, the 30th anniversary of the single market is a time of celebration for some and a time of sad remembrance for others. because three decades later, in this market, theoretically unique, offering, we are told, equal opportunities and responsibilities, there are member states that are still victims of the double standard. take my country, for example. although romania has been playing by the rules for 16 years, respecting the single market and european companies present on its territory, our economy does not benefit from all the development opportunities, because another member state is allowed to play dirty political games and to illegally oppose our entry into schengen. or romanian farmers, although hit today by the same crises as the rest of the union, plus the border war that brought them bankruptcy at the gate of the farm, they have to cope and be competitive, with subsidies that remain well below the european average. again for political reasons. on this anniversary day, therefore, i remind the council and the european commission that the double standard creates two distinct markets insofar as all have responsibilities but only some have opportunities.
mr president, we are celebrating 30 years of the single market. but what defines the single market? these are the four fundamental freedoms of the european union: freedom of movement, freedom of goods, freedom of capital and services. however, we see how two of these fundamental rights of romanians are being denied, because although since june 2010 the european commission has been asking the council to accept romania into the schengen area, through political games extremely alien to the european spirit, twenty million romanian citizens are being kept away from the free movement of the european union. it is unacceptable that a chancellor like neihammer, an anti-european, blackmails a country of 20 million romanian citizens. i have here a document published on december 19th, the last day of the european union council, which calls on austria to respect the schengen area. but here we are already discussing other matters which have nothing in common with the european spirit and i ask the swedish presidency to put the enlargement of the schengen area on the agenda.
mr president, commissioner, it is almost a year since the war between ukraine and russia led to the loss of human lives. and it's not just ukrainians and minorities who are dying there. my country helped, received millions of ukrainians and helped ukraine. what 's the reward ? president zelenskyy promulgates a law, although he was warned, and i took these steps, not to promulgate the law that prohibits minorities, romanians, over 400,000, from speaking and learning in the romanian language. yesterday, romania celebrated the day of culture and the day of its greatest poet, mihai eminescu, an internationally known poet, born in cernauti, which is now ukrainian land, once romanian. the romanians there couldn't organize anything, they can't talk. the romanian priests are getting their churches closed. i ask the european commission to verify the accession agreement. i voted for ukraine's accession agreement. there's a point there that calls for respect for minority rights. no longer does mr. president zelenskyy respect this. and let's not confuse war with minority rights. i dressed in white as a sign of peace, but i fight for my romanians there to have their rights respected.
mr president, dear commissioner, dear colleagues, considering the severe increase in adverse effects after the covid-19 vaccines, including many debts, i urge the european parliament and the european commission to create a big pharma victim reparation fund. similar funds were created for those who suffer from pollution of different companies under the principle that polluters pay. on 28 april 2021, in this plenary commissioner didier reynders said when he referred to the digital green certificate, and i quote, ‘we do not yet have full scientific evidence about the effects of the vaccines, but we cannot wait for all these questions to be answered to start taking action’. we see now the adverse effects of these vaccines, which without any doubt are creating many problems for many people, incapacitated many of them, and nevertheless killing some of them. i urge you, therefore, to support the creation of such a fund so the innocent people who were vaccinated can be compensated.
mr president, the 16th congress of the polisario front, the legitimate representative of the sarawi people, is currently being held in the dakhla refugee camp. for 50 years this people have waged a heroic struggle against the illegal occupation of their territory by the kingdom of morocco, which is imposing on western sahara the last colonization in africa, through violent repression and the imprisonment of activists who are fighting to end the illegal occupation of their homeland. we must put an end to this occupation by enforcing international law, the principles of the united nations charter, the relevant un resolutions and the agreements signed. the connivance of those who, like the european union and the united states, are in agreement with the disregard for the national rights of the sarawi people and the illegal exploitation of their wealth by the moroccan authorities must be stopped. we hereby express our full solidarity with the sarawi people and their cause, for the right to self-determination, liberation of the occupied territories and the guarantee of a free, independent and sovereign western sahara. certain that sooner or later the sarawi people will win.
mr president, i would like to raise a question which is a major problem for the internal market. it's the spread of livestock diseases and the conditions under which they are kept. last week, the czech republic confirmed its sixth outbreak of avian influenza this year. the incidence of avian influenza is the highest in its history in the european union. this outbreak in one of the large hive nurseries in the czech republic caused the loss of 750,000 nurseries. these hens laid up to 600,000 eggs a day, or 18.5 million eggs a month. it's not a big farm anymore, but it's just manufacturing. this example shows how risky animal meat farming is. in the big cities, of course, the flu will spread immediately. these chickens have lower immunity, higher resistance to antibiotics. i don't need to remind you that the conditions in cage-raised hens are unacceptable, where supposedly more should be done to prevent the spread of disease, but this is where these chickens were killed. so we're looking to the european commission to come up with new pan-european legislation to ban cage-breeding.
mr president, electricity is one of the essential products that cannot be missing from our lives at the present time, whether we are talking about our private or professional lives. the price of this energy is felt in all the products and services we need. the current russian-induced crisis has led to far too high energy prices, and hard-working people have become unable to afford a decent life. but we have to recognize that the energy market, as it is regulated at the moment, allows a very large margin for speculation. the increase is not entirely due to the gas crisis or the war russia has started in ukraine, and we should not accept the speculation that creates so many problems in society. market regulation needs to be reviewed and measures to keep the price of energy affordable for all citizens must be binding on all eu countries. because we are here, my dear colleagues, to protect all european citizens, regardless of the country or region in which they live.

for the greens/efa group mr president, the current situation is alarming and i understand the anger of the citizens. on the one hand, the increase in respiratory viruses puts european emergency services in an extremely precarious situation and, on the other hand, citizens are facing shortages of medicines, a direct consequence of the laissez-faire policy applied for years to the pharmaceutical industry. what was the result? massive relocation of drug production, shortages, patients not receiving the necessary care and a frightening lack of information for public authorities to act. by leaving the private sector in charge, public authorities, both national and european, have given way to the systematic commercialisation of public health. the result is the situation we have today, where the health of citizens is at risk. i would therefore like the commission and the council to comment on the measures they are planning, first in the short term, to support public health services immediately and to stock up on essential medicines, but also in the longer term to ensure more transparency in the drug production chains, so that public authorities can act upstream of shortages and not just in reaction. and finally, the possibility of creating a european public entity responsible for producing a minimum of essential medicines so that there is always a safety net.
for the group the left. mr president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, the problem is the system itself and all the changes we are making to it are purely cosmetic and do not solve it. the current legislative state allows for a number of measures that can at least mitigate drug shortages. let me remind you that the commission already came up with a new pharmaceutical strategy for europe years ago, in which it stressed the need to bring some of the pharmaceutical production back into the eu. however, neither it nor the member states have indicated who is to pay. and so for years nothing happens. there is also a need to discuss the general incorporation of medicines into european union law. today, medicines are treated as ordinary commodities, like sugar, salt and flour, and are therefore subject to the rules of the common market and the principle of supply and demand. and that just has to change! we need to eliminate drugs from the free movement of goods and regulate more movement. after all, i proposed it back in 2018. in some member states, they have now discovered to their horror that there are no central registers to allow a person to see in which particular pharmacies the medicine is available and in which it is not. a system to enable this has been proposed by myself and my colleagues - some of whom are sitting here - in the new ema mandate. again, this was rejected on the grounds that such a register would create an excessive administrative burden. then there is the absolutely unsatisfactory, even tragic, regulation of european union patent law, which is set up to produce only what is the highest margin. the right doesn't want to talk about it. so we just keep falling over, and we have and we will have drug shortages until we change the system. it's up to us, colleagues, the proposals here, it's up to us to have the courage to accept them.
mr president, ladies and gentlemen, italy is experiencing a temporary shortage of over 3 000 medicines and other member states are facing similar problems. the causes are not just increased demand for covid-19 waves, influenza viruses and respiratory infections, but also production bottlenecks related to higher energy costs, more expensive raw materials and supply chain difficulties. several european countries continue to have a problem of dependence on foreign countries for access to pharmaceuticals and medical devices. there is a concentration of production in third countries, with china and india at the forefront. it is therefore a priority to accelerate the achievement of the objectives of the european pharmaceutical strategy to help the sector increase production and autonomy in the union. immediate action should be taken with coordinated measures at union level to obtain, exchange and distribute medicines needed by each member state, to ensure strategic stocks and to combat any speculation in order to secure certain pharmaceutical treatments. don't leave anyone behind. we need a united and european response.

mr president, we see all these statistics coming from the european commission, from international organisations and from the governments of the member states, and they are worrying, as are the consequences. romania, for example, ended 2022 with a 22 percent increase in food prices, which translated into the gradual disappearance of some food products from stores and even the blocking of the european program for the distribution of fruits, vegetables, milk and dairy products in schools in almost half of the country. farmers simply could not keep up with prices and some of them stopped producing milk. the problem is that over 40% of romanian children are at risk of poverty and social exclusion, and the school lunch has cut off many from perhaps the only source of protein or even the daily food they had. and the forecast for 2023 shows us a 15% increase in the number of romanians living in absolute poverty. and i ask you, commissioner, do you see any real solution in solving this crisis that deprives children of the food they need?

member of the commission. madam president, ladies and gentlemen, the european objectives to achieve the green and digital transitions are well on track. technologically, renewable energy sources are more competitive than fossil fuels and co-legislators have taken the necessary steps to ensure that we reach the targets of 55% by 2030 and climate neutrality by 2050. until now, we may have lacked a thorough reflection on the industrial side of this transition, because this energy and climate transition will only be successful if it translates into a strong, competitive and job-creating european industry. we must jointly address the major risk of deindustrialisation we face, both by addressing the issue of energy costs, which is a driver, and by responding to the important support package that several of our partners have recently put in place, which is acting as a driver. i should like to say that my colleague thierry breton, who has had to leave us, has been warning us of this risk since september. the president of the commission set a very clear course yesterday on a law for a zero-emission industry, to support the emergence of a european clean technology industry. it also clearly called for a pragmatic approach to financing issues around an analysis of needs and european financing to address the risk of fragmentation of the internal market that state aid can generate. this funding could be divided into two phases: first, a bridge funding to act quickly and provide an immediate solution, and then a medium-term sovereignty fund, for a more structural solution in the context of the review of the financial perspective. the commission can count on the european parliament and the member states to strengthen the competitiveness of european industry and protect the integrity of the single market. today's debate confirmed the need to act quickly; in writing. we are now in a period of historic geopolitical change that should affect the way europe views the further development of european industry and supply chains. this is particularly important in the context of the need to make europe independent of energy supplies such as coal and gas from russia as soon as possible, but also to prevent new dependencies from being created with other parts of the world. in this respect, mention should be made of the import of components necessary for the production of, inter alia, photovoltaic panels or parts needed for the development of wind energy. the further development of european industry and the improvement of its competitiveness will require the production of large quantities of readily available energy and raw materials. at present, one of the main objectives of the union's energy policy is to become independent from russian gas, oil and coal supplies. for this reason, the union should diversify its sources of supply, but also focus on extracting and producing energy from the sources it has at its disposal, including nuclear energy, which guarantees affordable and stable electricity supplies. the role of coal and gas and the own resources of the member states will also be crucial during the transition period and the energy crisis.
in writing. – european policy to boost industrial competitiveness needs to be asserted more forcefully. practices built up over the years need to be overhauled. firstly, industrial manufacturing should be given first or equal priority with other sectors. secondly, all efforts must be unleashed to promote innovation and protect it when of european origin, without preventing the quick adoption of innovation developed outside the single market. thirdly, when industrial areas and projects of european importance have been identified, it should be ensured that they are really launched and not subjected to unnecessary delays. fourthly, better account should be taken of the demands of the green deal on industry in order to assist the latter satisfy such demands without losing competitiveness. as background to all this there must be some revision of state aid rules. our major partners have clear, strong and focused state aid programmes aimed at boosting their prioritised industrial sectors. while the integrity of the single market remains crucial, state aid rules should not be allowed to act as a drag on industrial investment and innovation. this is actually happening, as for semiconductors which supposedly is a priority area for the eu. unfortunately, from the perspective of industrial competitiveness, state aid might have become the tail that wags the dog.
in writing. in a shock of reality, eu forums are turning their attention from the chimera of the 'green transition' to the major challenges of european competitiveness, in a global environment marked by a multitude of crises, but also by protectionist measures by the us and china. it's a shame that it took the u.s. to take action for europe to realize that it was chasing green butterflies instead of stopping its deindustrialization. and what's worse is that instead of taking action, at least for now, the european commission is clinging to baseless blame on washington. the u.s. is doing what it needs to do to secure its industrial production base in the new geopolitical, technological and environmental context. brussels, on the other hand, is flying grandiose plans about 'zero pollution', with no response to our near-total dependence on imports of critical raw materials and a lack of factories for the industries of the future or a highly skilled workforce. the eu should be supporting its businesses in a concrete way, not debating the transformation of the unrealistic 'green deal' into a belated 'green deal industrial plan'. and if a negotiated solution with the us is really wanted, then it must be discussed at a high-level eu-us summit, not left to commission officials.

madam president, the 2021 pif report revealed that the number of cases of fraud and irregularities reported by the competent eu and national authorities remained stable in 2021 compared to 2020. but the value involved rose significantly – from eur 1.46 billion in 2020 to eur 3.24 billion in 2021 – and this is really worrying. detection of fraud and irregularities simply takes too long, averaging from two and a half to three years until they are reported to the commission. we need to work together in order to change this. the eppo and olaf are doing a very good job, but member states also have to improve their detection methods in order to decrease this significant delay. on 1 june 2021, the eppo finally started its operational activities and proved its capabilities and cost-effectiveness, despite the minimal resources and extreme time pressure. although the eppo’s budget for 2021 was less than eur 45 million, they managed to order the seizure of more than eur 250 million in their first year of operations. this means that we – in this house and at home – need to ensure full support and full speed to this institution. it is one of the best performing projects of europe so far. they need money, human resources and trust. let us give them the means and we will all succeed to better counter fraud in the european union.

madam president, the traditional fishery in the black sea is also threatened with extinction. on the romanian coast, as in the danube delta, only a few fishermen still use the ancient techniques, trying to keep this activity alive and to ensure the livelihood of their families and communities. but this way of surviving in perfect harmony with the environment is becoming increasingly complicated. it is a victim of coastal development, the fierce and unfair competition of fishing fleets from neighbouring countries and, more recently, the war in ukraine, which has forced small-scale fishermen to stop fishing. in the black sea, the danger is deadly because of the floating mines and the enormous russian arsenal in the vicinity of romania. our fishermen need help. today's report has solutions to this crisis such as easy access to european and national funding, the creation of local supply chains, better promotion of fishery products, attracting young people and women to the sector and improving working conditions for fishermen. but these measures really need to be implemented and they need to be implemented quickly if we want to be able to talk about traditional fishing in the black sea in the near future.
madam president, commissioner, i think this is an important moment, an important achievement for the european union, and i start from the four freedoms that define the single market - the free movement of persons, the free movement of goods, services and capital. and, of course, these are objectives that put the union on a very solid footing for future generations, but they draw attention to the fact that the two freedoms provided for in the treaty, such as the free movement of persons and goods, are seriously violated by states which, in the council of the european union, reject the enlargement of the schengen area, as happened at the jha council on 8 december last year, when romania and bulgaria were unjustifiably denied this right. the union will be strong when the single market is fully integrated.
madam president, i follow with great interest each year the report on human rights and democracy in the world. as a member of the subcommittee on human rights, i have the responsibility to closely monitor violations of the fundamental rights of individuals. and here i want to bring to your attention a situation that we know very little about, namely the situation of the citizens of the republic of moldova in the area occupied by the russian federation for more than 30 years. in the transnistrian region, every day, thousands of children face a regime that denies them the right to education. i am deeply grateful to them, to the teachers and principals of these schools who are trying to study in the state language of the republic of moldova, and the russian federation does not allow them to do so, because they are a serious case of human rights violation here in europe. more proof that russia has always defied and restricted fundamental freedoms.

the president, commissioner, colleagues. the operational priorities of the global gateway initiative for this year have been defined. the necessary budget is in place and the institutional framework and management have become operational. let's face it, it took a while to roll out the strategy, but it seems to have gone off the rails in terms of execution. we welcome that, but we want to see that this strategy is also given wide attention, in word and in deed. i mean that the political support is there, but what is needed now is to keep all the shareholders informed and mobilised, to keep all the institutions involved connected at european level and to keep the member states actively involved in the private sector. this union's connectivity and investment strategy is a very ambitious and very important undertaking, with a strong geopolitical dimension. we are rightly concerned about being a strong player on a global scale. well, then we need the right tools for that, too. which is why we need to invest in this strategy with every means at our disposal. just as we want to become more strategically independent from third countries, we also strengthen ourselves by working more and better with partners and allies. the paradox is interesting, but above all it teaches us that the global gateway initiative is an essential part of our common foreign policy in the end, including the security aspects. i therefore expressly hope that the strategy will take account of the challenges associated with systemic rivalries on a global scale in general and, of course, with the russian war against ukraine in particular. i am thinking in particular of the countries of the western balkans and our partners in the eastern and southern border regions.
mr president, madam commissioner, dear colleagues, for far too long we have been shy and at times naive about how we deployed our soft power as a union. we have funded projects all over the world, deployed often more development aid than any other foreign donor, and yet achieved little visibility for our investment and not enough political clout. and while we were doing all that, russia was pushing its narratives around the developing world and china was buying true influence and leverage with the belt and road. so yes, it was high time to become pragmatic in our use of money and truly strategic in how and where we spend it. but the global gateway’s success is in the way we implement it. we must map out our interests around the world, coherent to the new geopolitical realities. we must also do better in understanding the real needs of those that we put on this map, reading well the political context of each partner and the realistic space of negotiation and mutual benefit that can be achieved in the relationship. if a bridge is what the country needs, then let’s build a bridge. if it is a railway, then let’s build a railway. but at the same time we must also be agile enough to avoid that we build a bridge where china is buying a strategic harbour. what this means is that the global gateway cannot be implemented as a business—as—usual instrument with bureaucratic spreadsheets and formula. it requires constant political analysis and guidance, clever use of the member states’ historical knowledge and leverage in different countries and regions, and dynamic decision—making on where we spend and how we capitalise on investment. without that, it will just be more of the same.

in writing. both the s&d group and the esd have placed great emphasis on the need to reach tangible agreements on migration management before the end of the legislature. the european union is also calling for the establishment of a european union-wide network of specialised agencies for the protection of human rights and fundamental freedoms, including the european union's own specialised agencies for the protection of human rights and fundamental freedoms. we therefore stress the importance of focusing the efforts of the negotiations not on return but on improving the pathways for regular migration and on strengthening humanitarian channels for asylum seekers. in this way, not only will the humanitarian crisis be alleviated, but solutions will also be found to the demographic challenge and the shortage of labour in some sectors of the union's economy.
in writing. – on three major fronts, european policy on asylum and migration is either stagnant or deadlocked. what should be a common policy for regular migration to europe remains an undeveloped framework that if properly mounted could serve as a reasonable and effective way to counter illegal migration. ironically, europe needs migrants to refresh its demographic base and replenish labour markets. then, real tough action against people smuggling has been prioritised in verbal terms but ineffectually implemented. to be honest, the new initiatives that are being touted do not inspire confidence that there will be progress soon in dismantling the criminal networks involved. finally, solidarity between member states in sharing the burdens of incoming migration flows remains in limbo, caught in a stalemate which makes the commission’s attempts to achieve a compromise sound like diplomatic fudge. to be sure, eu action over the ukrainian refugee emergency has been admirable. but that very success has served to cloud the issues that need to be resolved over asylum and migration from africa and asia. additionally, it is fuelling perceptions outside the eu that europe applies discriminatory standards according to who arrivals are and where they come from. that hardly provides a good basis on which to deal with relevant third parties.
in writing. the open public consultation shows that a majority of citizens believe that publishers of political advertising should pay attention to transparency and fairness of reporting. political advertising services are expanding in the european union. the significant growth of such online services, in the face of uneven and fragmented regulation, has raised concerns that the internal market is not currently equipped to provide political advertising services that meet high standards of transparency in order to ensure a fair and open democratic process in all member states. such concerns about gaps and lack of transparency have already prompted some member states to take appropriate action. the national regulations on political advertising activities lay down obligations for political advertising service providers, which determine the accessibility of political advertising and indicate the elements of its content in order to ensure a high degree of transparency. such national rules help, inter alia, to ensure accountability and the overall organisation of a fair and open political process, including by helping to monitor the performance of political actors in their respective roles. rapid technological developments, increasing fragmentation of regulation and an increasingly problematic regulatory context, together with the increasing amounts of money spent on political advertising activities, indicate the need to take action to ensure the free movement of political advertising services across the union, while ensuring high standards of transparency, in order to make the electoral processes in the eu more open and fair.
the february european council must maintain the capacity for articulation and collaborative action which has enabled the european union to give a consistent response to major global challenges, and in particular to the invasion of ukraine and its impacts. the combination of policies in energy, economic support and monetary policy is beginning to show signs of being able to contain the inflationary peak and to crush growth prospects less. in this context, it is essential that the union move rapidly forward with the modernization and investment measures envisaged to maintain economic competitiveness in the face of the stimulus applied by the major competing powers and, in particular, by the united states of america. collaborative action must also have a strong social focus, protecting the most vulnerable european citizens and supporting the middle classes, in a logic of dignity, justice and prevention of populist advances. in the field of migration, it is essential to take clear measures, making asylum procedures more operational, cooperating with the countries of origin and opting for immigration corridors that are linked not only to the humanitarian dimension but also to the integration of migrants into the socioeconomic fabric of the union, where this is the will of both parties.
the rapporteur. madam president, madam minister, mr commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, the fact is clear: there is too much manipulation and abuse in our elections and in our democracies. foreign interference has become a plague. cambridge analytica, qatargate, and i could go on the context in which we find ourselves speaks for itself. as policy-makers, we must take all our responsibilities, provide appropriate solutions and responses to combat more effectively all forms of disinformation and interference in our democracies while preserving the openness that must always characterize the european public debate. this regulation was a first response to the increased demand for political transparency, a question which is obviously linked to the actions against interference that our assembly has been taking for several months in the special commission of inquiry of inge on disinformation and fake news. we are at a moment in our history when we must send a very strong message to our fellow citizens. we want more transparency, we want better protection against disinformation and foreign interference, we want to build a genuine single market for political advertising services - especially for our european smes - we want more legal certainty and fewer barriers, obstacles and bureaucracy for political advertising service providers. european political parties will no longer be blocked by 27 different regimes, but will have the opportunity to campaign more easily across borders. more market liberalisation, therefore, and more protection for our freedoms. we've all spoken out about the attacks on our democracies, the manipulation of social media, the dangers of fake news. we must ask ourselves whether we are serious about these intentions or whether we are just pretending. we have worked really well with all the groups in this parliament in the committee on the internal market. everyone has shown a sense of responsibility and cooperation, and i really hope that this work will be rewarded and supported by a very broad consensus in the plenary tomorrow. as regards the use of personal data for the targeting of political advertising and the lively debate surrounding it, i refer, of course, to article 12 and i will quote mark twain: "the rumours about the death of political advertising are greatly exaggerated. sorry, i should say: are widely amplified by some large platforms, very active, with their propaganda, recently, against our initiative. we will always fight for free speech for all, but not necessarily for the unregulated and unconsented amplification power of the big digital platforms. this is the aim, i believe, of the compromise on which my colleague anna donáth worked in the committee on civil liberties. in the cambridge analytica case, 87 million personal data was used without consent. the rules we want to introduce will ensure that no other cambridge analytica can happen. at the same time, these rules neither block nor interfere with personal political speech or the freedom of each of us. they only regulate content related to paid political advertising services. i would like to thank all my colleagues who have contributed greatly to the fact that this text is on the table today. thank you for your cooperation and thank you for your political will to work together. ladies and gentlemen, we have a unique opportunity to work towards a better democracy and a better european market. let's take her.
draft opinion of the consultative committee on culture and education. the commission has already made a number of proposals. disinformation campaigns and attempts to influence elections have also increased in europe. they are now a serious threat to our democracy. the regulation on the transparency of political advertising is not an easy one, but it is the right one and it is an important one. i believe that we in parliament have been able to find a balanced approach. while we have had many times in the offline world, more stringent measures through media regulation, through regulations and laws that have been raised at the national level to separate content and advertising cleanly, the online world has now been adapted accordingly. unpaid content is not covered, and free and critical journalism and political reporting and information are not advertising and therefore are not covered by this regulation. they are an essential part of our democracy, and this was particularly important to us in the committee on culture and education. we also take into account the special conditions of traditional media, because even though our lives are often digital nowadays, traditional media still enjoy great popularity and fulfil an important social role. they remain a reliable source of information and should not be overwhelmed by excessive bureaucracy. in a time when we are looking more closely, this regulation can give us all legal certainty and greater transparency and, above all, play an important role in protecting our democracy.
rapporteur for the opinion of the committee on civil liberties, justice and home affairs. – madam president, dear colleagues, i’m actually speaking today on behalf of my colleague anna donáth, who is the rapporteur on this file on behalf of libe and worked alongside the shadows to arrive to the result today. online targeting and ad delivery techniques have grown to be the business model for the big tech industry. it capitalises on our personal data, including very sensitive one, and not only the data that we consciously provide, but also the one that is inferred from our daily online activities. the use of such data creates specific audiences, fosters polarisation, causes deep divisions in our societies, risking the integrity of public debate, of electoral processes and ultimately of our democracies. as libe rapporteur, my colleague, anna donáth, is proud of the broad agreement and the solid text found in this house to counter these unhealthy practices and to protect our democracies and fundamental rights by restricting the use of those data. instead, we propose a system based only on consent on personal data provided by the users. additionally, this house worked hard to achieve an effective european enforcement mechanism with the european data protection board in the lead. our ep report gives protection to our citizens from the misuse of personal data and preserves european democracies from interference, manipulations and disinformation from malicious political actors.
member of the commission. – madam president, honourable members, thank you for the debate. i have noted your support, as well as concerns, and i will definitely convey them to the vice-president jourová. the commission really appreciates the fact that the parliament and the council share the main objective of the proposals. it remains the commission view that this regulation will enable an unprecedented high standard of transparency in the provision of the political advertising. it will also prevent the use of potentially problematic targeting methods. we have a chance to set global standards of transparency of the political advertising process and shouldn’t miss this opportunity. the commission supports your rapporteurs’ aims to present a negotiation mandate to the parliament to open the way for trilogues to begin fast. there is still time to get this done ahead of the european parliament elections next year, and we definitely stand ready to support you to ensure we do it well. thank you very much for this discussion.
the rapporteur. madam president, i cannot stress enough the relevance and importance of this dossier in the current political context. with tomorrow's vote, we must have a very strong position, a very large majority in the european parliament, to start the trilogues properly, and we have no time to lose if we want these new rules to be implemented before the next european elections. i am convinced that once this text is in force, elections in the european union will be more transparent and more resilient, as all my colleagues in the renew group have said. of course there is no risk to independent media and journalism that, mrs verheyen also explained very well. ladies and gentlemen, the balance sheet is clear: you know very well that the platforms simply have to check the existence of political sponsors and whether the information they provide about them is complete. we will not introduce any general monitoring obligation, let alone any form of censorship. but as paul tang said, in the end, it's the voters who have to decide, not the digital platforms. daniel freund and alexandra geese have rightly pointed out that greater transparency is essential to protect our democracies and maria-manuel leitão-marques is right on this point: the european database we are going to introduce with our proposal will undoubtedly help in this. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the directive is implemented. it is unlikely that our candidates or elected representatives will be made to suffer more in local elections; i do not see any danger in that, given the proportionality of the obligations we are going to introduce. so we are giving a very important and long-awaited answer. let us go on, ladies and gentlemen: what we are doing is common sense.
in writing. i support the aim of the regulation to establish harmonised rules on political advertising in the eu internal market. in political advertising, it's important to know who paid for the ad. i am opposed to the interference of third countries in our political systems and to any malicious attempts to manipulate people. transparency requirements for political advertising need to be strengthened. i disagree with article 12 of the bill, which deals with the targeting of political advertising and the prohibition of it. after the facebook/cambridge analytica scandal, it's clear to everyone that there is an inconsistency in democracy where some interest groups have the ability to manipulate even the subconscious mind of a person and others don't have access to those tools. in a democratic election, all candidates should have equal opportunities. i don't think we should go back to pre-internet times when it comes to targeting political advertising, but there should be a level playing field for all to target political advertising, and the scope of the targeting should be clearly defined.
written. the objective of this regulation is to harmonise transparency obligations for political advertising service providers and to introduce harmonised rules on political advertising. it is important to emphasise that the new regulation will apply to all types of elections: elections to the european parliament, as well as all elections or referendums organised at national, regional and local level in the member states and elections to determine the leadership of political parties. our group has been at the forefront of advocating for stricter rules on transparency of political advertising to help citizens recognise political ads and exercise their democratic rights in an informed way, strengthening their trust in democracy and elections in the eu. the implementation of this regulation will help us to combat disinformation and prevent the illegal interference of other states in our electoral processes. by enforcing this regulation, we protect our democracy.
in writing. i support the ambitious agreement reached at committee level to increase transparency rules on paid political advertising in eu countries. paid political advertising must be clearly marked/labeled and provide a number of key information, including the name of the sponsor, the amount spent, the provenance of the funds used, the link between the advertising and the relevant elections or referendums, and the period for which the publication and dissemination are intended. citizens need to know why they are seeing an advertisement, who paid for it and how much, and which population groups were targeted. governments or national parliaments will have to adopt transparency regulations on the spending of party money. i draw attention to the fact that public money should be used to present the projects and public policies of the parties and not to attack political opponents.
in writing. disinformation campaigns and attempts to influence elections have increased in europe and have become a serious threat to our democracies. that is why i welcome the regulation of political advertising which will help strengthen the democratic process. we have insisted on clearer rules for political advertising, and through our efforts, the european registry for online political advertising will be implemented, which will significantly contribute to increasing transparency. we don't want the regulation to restrict the fundamental right of freedom of expression, we don't equate political opinions with political advertising. we want to protect citizens from techniques used online, especially in the electoral context, which often threaten citizens' personal data. these amendments also aim to limit the power of actors outside the union to influence the democratic process and we will have an important tool to combat disinformation. it will allow us to avoid foreign intervention, through clearly defined rules. in this context, i have also proposed to strengthen european political parties by promoting their logos in national advertising. we must respect freedom of expression but at the same time we must protect our citizens from manipulation techniques.
in writing. the grey areas of political advertising that permeate the heart of democratic processes, including elections and referendums, are too numerous to ignore. that's why i'm in favor of stricter rules on political advertising. that is why i supported the amendments concerning the rules on political advertising in the eu. these aim to increase the transparency and traceability of political advertising, make more information available to the public about the advertising clients, ban so-called micro-targeting, advertising and advertising financing in the eu for non-eu entities and impose stricter sanctions on infringers. i support the amendments also in the light of the memory of the bad practices in slovenia. before each election, we have unfortunately witnessed many bad practices in political advertising, which avoid transparency or ignore it altogether. actions like this are starting to make people trust politicians. there is a widespread belief that politics is driven by some backgrounds whose interests are more than benevolent. the revised rules on political advertising will provide clear legal provisions on the transparency, traceability and openness of political advertising. the changes adopted will severely restrict marginalisation practices and protect democratic processes in the eu.
in writing. political advertising services in the eu are constantly evolving, especially in the online space, and such services are often also provided across borders. the digital transformation and technological changes have enabled the proliferation of various new media and methods of financing, preparing, placing, promoting, publishing and disseminating political advertising. this development, in the context of fragmented and unevenly enforced national regulation, clearly demonstrates that internal market rules should be adapted to ensure an adequate level of transparency of political advertising, which is necessary for a fair and open electoral process in all member states. therefore, at a time when new digital technologies are becoming more widespread and social networks are being used to disseminate political advertising, it is essential to lay down harmonised transparency obligations for political advertising service providers and to introduce harmonised rules on the use of targeting and leveraging techniques for political advertising where personal data are used.
president-in-office of the council. – madam president, honourable members, commissioner, thank you for inviting me, the presidency, to join this debate. the fight against money laundering and terrorist financing is a key task for our presidency and connects to one of our four priorities: security. this is also an area where the council is firmly engaged. last december, the council reached an agreement on the negotiation mandates for the anti-money laundering regulation and the anti-money laundering directive. in addition to that, we have a mandate for negotiating the substance of the anti-money laundering authority. moreover, on the transfer of funds regulation, we have, together with you, reached an agreement, which is now in the final stage before adoption. i look forward to engaging with the european parliament on the remaining negotiations as soon as you are ready. but let me come to the issue on hand – the commission delegated act on the eu list of high-risk third-countries for anti-money laundering and terrorist financing purposes. as is always the case, the council stands ready to examine any list put forward by the commission. the commission adopted this delegated act on 19 december. after this, we initiated without delay an internal process to assess member states’ intentions regarding the act. however, given the date of adoption, the period for this assessment largely overlapped the holiday period in our member states. this is especially relevant because the scrutiny period for this delegated act is one month as opposed to the normal three months for financial market regulations. because of this, the objection period was extended. this was later confirmed by the council on 17 january, and therefore the deadline to object is now 20 february and we are currently assessing member states’ intentions regarding the delegated act. our current understanding is that the council is not interested in objecting to the delegated act. for the council’s examination, we are taking into consideration the lessons learned from the 2019 update of the list. we believe that, since then, the commission has worked hard to improve the method and has engaged closely with member states, which was appreciated by the council. i would like to conclude by reaffirming the presidency’s full commitment to maintaining close cooperation between the institutions and the money laundering area. i look forward to hearing your views. thank you for your attention.
member of the commission. – madam president, honourable members, i am pleased to speak to you today on this important issue on behalf of my colleague, commissioner mcguinness. for this commission, as for this house, the fight against money laundering and the financing of terrorism is a top priority. we need to keep strengthening our defences against money laundering and terrorist financing. this means we must also address risks that come from outside the eu. the anti—money laundering directive requires the commission to list high—risk third country jurisdictions. these jurisdictions have strategic deficiencies in their national frameworks on anti-money laundering and countering the financing of terrorism. under the directive, any third—country jurisdiction publicly identified as a risk to the international financial system by the financial action task force is also presumed to be a risk to the eu internal market. this is justified because the international financial system is highly interconnected. market operators are closely connected. our market is very open, and there is a high volume of cross—border transactions to or from the union. against this background, the eu list of jurisdictions must be aligned with the list published by the task force. this is a legal requirement that was decided by the co—legislators when they agreed the anti—money laundering directive. the commission therefore adopted the delegated act after a full analysis of the situation, and according to all the information at our disposal. we respect the position of the council that they need more time to scrutinise the act – and this is the right, of course, of the co—legislator. let me underline that this updated list is essential for the integrity of our financial system and for the eu’s commitment to combat money laundering globally. we remain confident that it will enter into force as soon as possible. finally – and this is very important for me – let me add that the commission, together with the european external action service and the eu delegations concerned, work closely with jurisdictions that are already listed or soon will be. we help them address shortcomings in their national anti—money laundering and countering terrorism financing frameworks. we provide them with technical assistance to help them fully implement their action plans agreed in the task force, because listing is not the end but the start of a process. the aim is to help jurisdictions improve their anti—money laundering frameworks. we regularly de—list jurisdictions who make improvements, as indeed we are doing in this same act. the commission will continue to work closely with this house and keep you fully informed of future developments. i look forward to hearing your views.
i am speaking on behalf of the ppe group. the commission has already made a number of proposals. the european union has had a money laundering problem for years. estimates put the volume of suspicious transactions within europe in the triple-digit billions. this is partly an imported problem. what is clear is that our defence against money laundering and terrorist financing is only as strong as the weakest link in the chain. it is therefore right that we look very closely at third countries with obvious deficits. that is why we, as the european parliament, have co-initiated the list of high-risk third countries, and it must of course be kept up to date, there is no doubt about that. but it is also true that we have a great task ahead of us in the area of money laundering, also within the european union. in the member state i know best, we have a special money laundering unit based at customs, which is moving a huge mountain of unresolved suspicions. we're talking about over 50,000 suspected cases that haven't been processed. if only one in a hundred of the suspected cases is really a money laundering or terrorist financing problem, then it is obvious that a lot of things go wrong if they are not properly dealt with. so before we make a scandal here because the council sometimes needs a month longer - we sometimes need that too - let us concentrate on ensuring that the council does not remove a country from the list. then we're on the right track.
on behalf of the renew group. mr president, in july 2022, following the revision of the fatf list, which included the uae in the list of jurisdictions subject to closer money laundering monitoring, i sent a written question to the commission regarding the inclusion of the uae in the european list of high risk third countries on anti-money laundering. in december, the commission proposed to revise this list and include the uae along with other countries, but the council did not consider this a priority and postponed discussion of this issue. on the other hand, today, after so many scandals with corrupt dictators and money laundering that have affected the union, we have not clearly and firmly banned passports and golden visas in the union. i would like to believe that we are not turning a blind eye to anti-money laundering issues at home or in third jurisdictions, especially as we know very well that russia is circumventing eu sanctions with the help of some of these jurisdictions. i ask the council why we are still reflecting today? why do we have no courage, only interests? if not now, when?

in writing. the deterioration of the humanitarian situation in afghanistan is staggering. however, on humanitarian aid - a subject of particular interest to me in my committee on development - the european union cannot bow to the dictates of the taliban. let's talk about the inexorable decline in women's status. in late december, the taliban banned female ngo staff from working in afghanistan. this decision is not surprising. it does, however, mean that many afghans will not have access to western aid, for example in the medical field. it is easy to understand that the european union, which in 2021 promised to almost quadruple its humanitarian aid to the country, may, in doing so, condone such practices. moreover, the debate over whether to classify the taliban as a terrorist organization continues to shake canada, demonstrating that fears about potential aid diversion remain acute. in short, the european union must clarify its position on a regime that europeans reject and which is decidedly at odds with their values. thank you.

mr president, ladies and gentlemen, in its judgment of 19 january last, the court of justice of the european union banned the use of neonicotinoids in the coating of beet seeds. this decision puts french beet growers in a very worrying situation. one in four beet growers have already indicated their willingness to throw in the towel and move to other crops. in the short term, therefore, french sugar production is likely to collapse. the french minister for agriculture has just undertaken to compensate 100% for any losses in beet production due to the lack of treatment. in reply to my question, the commissioner for agriculture said that he would accept this exceptional national aid. i therefore hope that these commitments will be respected in order to encourage french beet growers to continue their production.

mr president, ladies and gentlemen, commissioner, the right to freedom of opinion and expression is one of the most important democratic rights in the european union. but the right to an opinion is not the right to be misinformed, nor is it the right to present one's own opinions as absolute truths. it's not the right of extremists to promote hatred, to lie to people. online communication has made it much easier and faster for misinformation to spread to people who don't know or can't verify the information. what's even worse is that this misinformation is also being spread through the mainstream media, where there are news channels in all the member countries of the european union that seem to follow no rules anymore except for financial interest. disinformation is extremely dangerous for democracy, because it exploits people's good faith and changes behavior in social relationships, leading to a high degree of polarization, to hatred. we must take this matter very seriously, colleagues, and as soon as possible.

mr president, we are talking about transparency and targeting of political advertising, and this is a very important subject because it has consequences for the democratic process and for the future of the european union. this file imposes a series of restrictions on political parties, especially with regard to electoral processes, in order to reduce the 'popularisation' of society to which the online environment has contributed significantly. but our response should not be a series of restrictions and bans that ignore modern communication. our attention should be focused on educating citizens, so that they are able to recognize fake news and attempts at manipulation. i think that it is not the democratic processes that will lead to polarization and that the subject is not sufficiently debated.
mr. president, i don't think we need to convince anyone of the importance of the regulation we voted on today. transparency in politics is essential, even more so in political advertising. transparency in politics and the relationship with citizens must be one of deep respect, and i think that from this point of view it is essential that we do everything we can to combat foreign interference of manipulation and disinformation. we have seen, especially in recent years, the dramatic consequences that these can produce. and that's why, because technological advances are allowing foreign state actors and others to interfere in our political affairs, in our political actions, it's essential that we prepare as well as possible, especially as the european elections are coming up next year.
mr president, in the european single market we are dealing with many large companies, with thousands of employees in different countries, with different laws, with different pay levels and with a variety of tools for consultation and protection of employees. however, regardless of the state in which they work, these employees are subject to the same decisions of the parent companies. european works councils thus play a key role in respecting workers' rights, in particular their right to be informed and consulted on decisions affecting them. that is why we need to ensure that we have a rigorous legal framework at european level and that the european works councils directive is updated to meet new realities. we must, however, protect the employees of community-scale undertakings by ensuring that the works councils actually function and by imposing strict sanctions on companies which do not comply with their obligations to consult them. that is why i voted to revise this directive.
the president shall draw up a report in respect of the implementation of this decision and shall request that a review be carried out of the directive on the laws of the european works council. the review is what we're looking forward to. it is important that laws and regulations are transposed into national law and that all european companies operate from the czech republic to the czech republic. we welcome the report which has been taken by the european works council to adapt the directive on the approximation of the laws of the member states relating to the labelling of foodstuffs to the requirements of the directive. this law, this balance between the former and the present. that week, there was a sea-change in the uk, which had not happened in years. i therefore hope that the european union will not be the first to review these laws without a vote, nor will it be the last to do so, as mr wallace and mr daly said, but we will be the last to do so.
mr president, i also supported this report. i think it balances the position of employees in the desired direction with respect to the largest companies where the position of employees has not been very strong. we have also had a recent experience in the czech republic where a korean company blocked collective bargaining and even refused to let protesting employees into the factory. i think these are examples where it is clear that the existing directive needs to be deepened. i therefore supported it also because it contains a number of interesting proposals, procedures, as well as case law and the approach of this european works council to the courts, to allow the blockage to be removed and the negotiations to be moved forward. so, from my point of view, it is a request for a deepening of the existing directive.
