blue-card reply. – thank you so much for this question, and thank you so much for offering me the opportunity to clarify the fact. i worked – before i was elected here – as a programmer, so i know exactly how this data is stored, so when you are claiming right now that you are providing access or control of the citizens over the data, that is false – because that data is not stored on their device and they do not have physical control over the data. the data is stored on a server or on a cloud. what this regulation does is allowing or granting access to any governmental agency or even eu institutions to access that data directly. that’s what the report says.
madam president, commissioner, i believe that after the health crisis, the economic crisis, the energy crisis, the security crisis, we are facing great challenges and i believe that it is essential to focus our attention on solving the problems facing our societies so that the recommendations of the european semester are applied exactly as they are intended to create a much more effective coordination to overcome all the vulnerabilities that the member states of the european union are going through. because the inflation, the social crises, the economic crises, the financial crises that europe is going through now can only be overcome through better coordination, and it is essential that member states, together with the commission, implement the necessary reforms so that our societies stop wasting money, focus on the vulnerabilities that citizens face when they want stability, better-paying jobs, and this can only be achieved through smart investment. europe has to face all the challenges it is facing now, and obviously we can only do this with smart investment and, of course, better coordination, so that competitiveness is the key word and we can defend our investments and, of course, plan for a future based on economic growth.
madam president, ladies and gentlemen, commissioner, council, the social pact between the institutions and the citizens is broken. the rules at european level are incapable of responding to the social crisis and, in fact, in their rigidity, they are frustrating the efforts made in recent years to achieve a genuine economic, territorial and social recovery. the european semester, like the stability and growth pact, is an outdated instrument that is an obstacle to social convergence. the principles of the european pillar of social rights have remained a dead letter. that is why we greens are calling for a reform of the economic governance framework, mandatory targets for the eradication of poverty and social exclusion and an assessment of the social impact of the plans of the various member states and of the money allocated by national governments. without the necessary fiscal space to get back to investing in education, health and inclusion measures, such as the minimum income, there can never be a just transition. the only chance we have is to start from social justice and we greens have clear ideas: it needs reform.
madam president, commissioner, colleagues, in this crisis situation, people and only people may be at the forefront. the employment report calls on the member states to rapidly implement the provisions established in the minimum wage directive, saying that decent wages are an essential tool in helping households keep up with rising energy prices. let's take some examples of how these ideas are implemented. concerning my country, the commission launched the opinion that latvia has made limited progress with regards to the structural part of the fiscal recommendations. how does this limited progress look like? the minimum wage in latvia in 2023 was raised by 24% while remaining the quite low eur 620. meanwhile, the salary of the chairman of parliament has been raised by 75%, minister president by 53%, members of parliament by 34%. the salary of chairmen, therefore, is 13 times bigger than the minimum salary. really decent wages for the few and unworthy for the many. i think that the semester process needs a more thorough look at the elements of dispersion of wages and the just effective fight against poverty in general with a special focus on in-work poverty, direct support of most vulnerable groups. this is the solution which we need.
madam president, dear commissioner, dear minister, i need to observe right now that we have a lot of young people that are watching us right now, and they are visitors and there are much more of them than us as members of parliament or the commission or the council in this room. and this is a pity because they are really interested about this shift that we are talking about. the change of the semester. the semester is the way that people, the young people, everyone, are looking to see how we are shifting towards a sustainable, inclusive and resilient growth model. this debate is crucial. this is not just a technocratic thing that we’re doing here. the future of investment, if we want to do investment, cannot be foreseen without a social component that responds to the fears and expectations of the young generations and all the citizens in europe. we have to fight against all forms of social exclusion and poverty, especially poverty among children. the recovery and resilience plans are essential for supporting smart reforms and investments. and then the review that we are talking about of this semester, it’s a unique opportunity to redesign the architecture of eu economic governance and to create a tool for reinforcing reforms and investment. we look forward to working together to create a stronger and more resilient europe. and we also look forward to working with the young generations that right now are interested in knowing more about europe.
madam president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, the economic and political choices we make in the coming months will be decisive for future generations. the way forward for the revival of european competitiveness is not through higher interest rates, which have already had a negative impact on consumers, households and businesses, but through a sound industrial and trade policy. the establishment of a european sovereign fund will have to be the pivot on which the new programming will have to be developed. but first of all, it is imperative to review the rules of the budgetary policies of the member states, which are obviously no longer suited to the current socioeconomic context. the commission's communication, although it is a first step in the right direction, is still far from the needs of the states and, above all, presents several knots to be untied. i refer in particular to the scope, the power of the commission and the imposition of a multiannual ceiling on public expenditure. in fact, i agree with prime minister giorgia meloni that the future stability and growth pact should take greater account of the issue of balancing these two values: stability and growth, both necessary. we therefore hope that the commission proposal will not include public investment in innovation, research and infrastructure in the deficit.
madam president, under the guise of economic coordination, the european semester in brussels pursues its ultra-liberal aims and imperial aims. for example, the commission recommends softly euphemistically to france to harmonise another euphemism our pension system. in other words, to abolish our schemes specific to certain occupations which are justified by the burdensome nature of these occupations or by the demographic situation between beneficiaries and contributors, or sometimes even both, and i am thinking in particular of the schemes for our seamen. we definitely don't have the same values. the european union is defending the right of peoples to self-determination on the other side of the world, but it is blatantly trampling on popular sovereignty in brussels. it is true that mrs von der leyen's predecessor said: 'there is no democracy in the face of treaties'. well, in france, there is only one sovereign, and that is the people.
madam president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, i would like to point out a few issues which, in my view, need to be addressed urgently and which are in line with the dozens of discussions i have had recently with european citizens in romania and other countries. people expect concrete results from the european union to help them live better. but today we have a pandemic that's affected them, we have the russian invasion of ukraine that's affected, we have rising energy prices that's affected, just as unemployment and lack of real opportunity affects us every day. a lot of people don't know how they're going to pay their utilities, they don't know if they're going to have a job, they don't know if they're going to pay their mortgages. that's why we're very interested in having solutions now and not in 2025 or 2050. the small companies that have to lay people off or have to close their doors, what can we say to them at this point? what concrete solutions are we offering them now, beyond good intentions and plans for the next decade? jobs don't just come out of nowhere. they are created by entrepreneurs, by people who need to be supported and in no way burdened by new versions of legislation or by the bureaucracy of the european union. if firms and entrepreneurs disappear or are tempted to go to america or asia, how will we generate quality jobs and from what money will we provide the necessities that european citizens need? that's why it's so important that we invest in people, first of all in programs and entrepreneurs that will create those high-paying jobs for citizens.
madam president, ladies and gentlemen, the truth is that we can say that the political line chosen by the european union in this last mandate has put us in a compromised situation, with foreseeable errors, warned by groups like mine. we see jobs being destroyed, our business fabric losing density and industry not finding the conditions in our environment to develop its activity. environmental demands have led to shortages and rising energy prices, inflation and legal uncertainty. this situation is what is generating poverty and misery, which cannot be solved by social policies alone. because there must be a balance between stability and growth; we must support it while generating prosperity, creating the environment that allows us to be competitive, to encourage entrepreneurship and to revive our economy. it is surprising that, at a time when we must re-examine and re-order our priorities, we should be proposing a leap forward, to the point of calling for the recommendations of the european semester to lose their economic focus and to involve interference in areas where the union has no competence, with an ideological vision which has already had and continues to have devastating effects where it is applied.
madam president, however much they may wish to paint it as social or green, the european semester will remain what it has always been: an instrument of conditionality and interference in the budgetary policy of the states, to the detriment of the peoples, in favour of the great powers and economic groups. it should be recalled that the country-specific recommendations for 2022, in the case of portugal, showed the strengthening of budgetary pressures until 2023 aimed at containing current state expenditure, i.e. pressing towards the containment of real wages and investments in public services. this discussion cannot be separated from the announcement of the end of the escape clause in the stability pact and the re-introduction of the excessive deficit procedures, coupled with the ongoing debate on the revision of the so-called economic governance, aimed at increasing the constraints on public spending and investment by states and their sanctioning framework. the negative developments are anticipated to the detriment of countries such as portugal.
member of the commission. – madam president, members, our growth strategy is based on the four dimensions of competitive sustainability. it remains the compass for coordinating eu economic and social policies both in the short and medium term. and the framework for doing so is the european semester, and i fully agree this should not be a technocratic exercise. this should be an exercise — a political one, a democratic one — based on realities. by integrating next generation eu as well as repowereu in the european semester we have shown that the eu can both be agile and flexible in responding to the important challenges and persistent uncertainty which we all face. the eu has, for the time being, considerable resources which should be used in an efficient and targeted way to precisely respond to these challenges, also those challenges coming from competitors outside the eu. and each new payment reflects steady progress in meeting milestones and targets and implementing key reforms and investments. the next weeks and months will also be crucial to shape the future of our fiscal rules. and, while the commission has proposed a bridging solution for ’24, it is urgent to agree on a revised set of fiscal rules. the commission will present legislative , and we rely on the european parliament to play an active role in reaching a rapid agreement because it is important to guarantee stability, to allow reforms and enhance investments. this is the key for successful and socially fair transitions. effective employment and social policies are key to enable fair transitions and to protect and accompany people. we need to ensure that the eu is at the forefront of the industrial transformation aimed at ending the dependency on fossil fuels, but also improving its competitiveness and creating quality jobs. the effective coordination of economic employment and social policies in the context of a strong european semester remains key to succeed. and this should also be reflected in a reformed economic governance, as asked by many speakers.
president-in-office of the council. – madam president, honourable members, commissioner, many of you speakers, for example, mr ferber and mr van overtveldt raised the issue of competitiveness and the lack thereof. it was said that we need to mend the roof while the sun is shining. now, when it has begun to rain, this is even more pressing. as was said, the solution is not only more money and more subsidies; the lack of european competitiveness goes deeper. that is why the swedish presidency is prioritising long-term competitiveness. the european semester is an important element of this. to attract investments, stability and predictability is key. the european semester is instrumental to achieve this. allow me also to assure you once more that the council gives great attention to the european semester. you have touched on some important points today, and your ideas will make a valuable contribution to the ministerial debate on this european semester, which continues in the council. as i also said earlier, we must step up our efforts to implement the digital and green transition, and it requires swift, targeted and coordinated policy actions both at the eu and the national levels to carry out structural reforms. i would also like to emphasise the importance of also engaging with our citizens to increase national ownership. i welcome the initiatives taken in this regard, such as dialogues and consultations with stakeholders, civil society and social partners in member states. today’s debate has shown that, despite some differences, there is a lot of common ground between us on which we should build. in a union of highly integrated systems, enhanced economic and social policy coordination can help prevent discrepancies and contribute to ensuring convergence and stability in the eu as a whole and in its member states. i am therefore confident that we will continue to work together to find appropriate solutions to meet the challenges before us.
the joint debate is closed. the vote will take place tomorrow, wednesday 15 march 2023. the commission shall be assisted by the committee of the regions.
the next item is the oral questions to the council on the adequate minimum income for the active inclusion of dragoş pîslaru, krzysztof hetman, estrella durá ferrandis, lucia ďuriš nicholsonová, sara matthieu, giuseppe milazzo, özlem demirel, on behalf of the committee on employment and social affairs (o-000050/2022 - b9-0008/2023) and to the commission on the adequate minimum income for the active inclusion of dragoş pîslaru, krzysztof hetman, durá ferrandis, lucia ďuriš nicholsonová, matthieu, giuseppe milazzo, özlem demirel, on behalf of the committee on employment and social affairs (o-000050/2022 - b9-0008/2023).
author. – mr president, dear commissioner, dear minister, living in dignity is a human right. in 2021, 95.4 million people in the eu, representing 21.7% of the eu population, were at risk of poverty and social exclusion. just to clarify, that means that one in five people in the eu are in poverty or at risk of social exclusion. the covid-19 pandemic and the rising cost of living further exacerbated the plight of individuals and households in precarious socioeconomic situations. minimum income schemes serve as social safety nets, guaranteeing a minimum standard of living and the right to dignity at all stages of life. for people lacking sufficient resources and decent, stable employment, access to adequate minimum income plays an essential role in reducing poverty, social exclusion and inequalities and such schemes should be combined with incentives and measures enabling labour market activation for people who are able to work. why do we have this debate? we have this debate because the situation in europe is such, that there are many member states that do not have such provisions. i myself, as a minister of labour in 2016 initiated the law on the minimum income for inclusion, it has not been implemented in 2017, and it should, and it has been postponed. it is still not implemented today. that is why we need this debate. representing the committee of employment and social affairs, i would like to ask the council the following questions: how will the council encourage member states to gradually set their minimum income schemes to at least the level of the national at risk of poverty threshold while providing incentives to reintegrate people who can work into the labour market? how will member states ensure that beneficiaries of such schemes are not forced to accept precarious work? how does the council intend to improve the coverage of the existing national scheme? how will member states tackle the non-take-up problem – a very serious problem? what kind of mechanism will they establish to raise the awareness and ensure that those eligible are receiving the support that they can actually benefit from it? how will the member states ensure that that such minimum income schemes do not substitute for social policy measures in other areas such as housing, healthcare, disability support or labour market integration? and how are member states cooperating with social partners, civil society and all the other relevant stakeholders on the design, implementation and monitoring of the minimum income schemes, and where do they see room for improvement? the committee of employment and social affairs would also like to ask the commission a couple of questions. how does the commission intend to ensure that member states gradually increase their minimum income schemes to at least the international arope threshold while providing the incentives that i have referred to before and enabling the measures to reintegrate those who can work in the labour market? then the question is how will the commission monitor the implementation of this recommendation and the contribution to achieving the 2030 targets, in particular, reducing the number of people at risk of poverty and social exclusion by at least 15 million, including the 5 million children – we are, right now, close to two years after porto. how will it help member states to tackle the non-take-up problem? similar question for the council as well. and what action does it intend to promote non-discrimination in the context of the minimum income schemes? and last but not least, certainly, how will it involve the social partners at european level in implementing and monitoring the council recommendation? these are the questions that stem from the debate that we had in the committee of employment and social affairs. you will hear my colleagues underlining other very important elements in that. but i am really glad that we have this opportunity to discuss about this very, very serious situation. and i will thank you very much for your pertinent answers.
on behalf of the vice-president of the commission/high representative of the union for foreign affairs and security policy. – mr president, honourable members, i think that this debate has demonstrated that we all share the wish to see georgia succeed in its reforms and on its european path. and we all need to help this to be delivered for the people of georgia, because this is the aim of the people of georgia and this is also the key to any eu accession process – to deliver on the 12 priorities because those 12 priorities are setting out the most important tasks in front of the country. and now we need georgia to deliver, to work hard on these reforms, and it is the only way for the country to advance on its chosen european path. to the question of how we support this work and how we help them to deliver, of course, we are standing by financially; we are standing by with experts. but make no mistake: this can only be delivered by the government of georgia and by the parliament of georgia, because again, this is all for the georgian people.
the debate is closed.
the next item is the debate on the statement by the vice-president of the commission / high representative of the union for foreign affairs and security policy on the problems facing the republic of moldova (2023/2595 ((rsp)).
on behalf of the vice-president of the commission/high representative of the union for foreign affairs and security policy. – mr president, honourable members of the european parliament, today’s debate is a very timely one as russia and its proxies are actively working to destabilise the pro-reform government of moldova. since the start of russia’s illegal invasion of ukraine, moldova has been facing the consequences of russia’s brutal aggression just tens of kilometres away from its borders. it has been confronted with a growing number of challenges, such as loss of key markets, soaring inflation, violating of its airspace and staged protests. nevertheless, moldova has shown remarkable solidarity with the refugees fleeing ukraine. what is happening now? according to the moldovan authorities, the probability of direct military aggression against moldova is currently low. moreover, the de facto authorities in the transnistria region want to avoid being drawn into russia’s war of aggression. but vigilance is necessary due to the presence of russian troops and ammunition in the transnistria region. over the last months, there have been increasing russian attempts to destabilise moldova with hybrid actions. the president of moldova, maia sandu, has warned about plans to attempt a change of power through violent means. russia’s lies about ukraine massing troops to attack moldova’s pro-russian separatist region of transnistria were swiftly denied by chişinău. at the same time, high energy prices and high inflation have increased the burden on the population, worsening their living conditions. russia has used, again, energy supplies as a political weapon. but the moldovan government, with the eu’s support, has managed to tackle these unprecedented challenges. it is remarkable that despite all the pressures, moldova now is an eu candidate country and it continues working on reforms that aim to bring the country closer to the eu. and in that regard, on 2 february this year, commission services provided further policy guidance on eu acquis-related reforms in our analytical report. this is to complement our opinion of last year on the country’s application for eu membership to be followed by a comprehensive assessment of moldova’s progress in meeting the eu accession criteria later on this year in the autumn. honourable members, let me stress that moldova remains on top of the eu’s political agenda with our unwavering support to the country’s security, territorial integrity and sovereignty. over the last 12 months, the eu has put together a comprehensive support package worth more than eur 1 billion to strengthen moldova’s resilience. moreover, the economic and investment plan for our eastern partners has a significant potential, which needs to be fully tapped to support moldova’s reform agenda. the five flagship initiatives we agreed together aim to unlock eur 1.6 billion of investments for moldova. we are supporting the modernisation of moldova’s defence sector with the support of the european peace facility by providing eur 47 million. we will effectively double moldova’s national defence budget. high representative vice-president borrell has proposed to eu member states to maintain a substantial level of financial assistance to moldova also this year. additionally, eu experts are supporting moldova on cybersecurity, addressing hybrid threats and countering disinformation. the eu also launched a hub to address internal security and border management challenges. and we are looking at further ways to support moldova. high representative vice-president borrell is taking forward the work on preparing a possible eu-civilian csdp mission to further strengthen moldova’s security and resilience. the deployment of such a mission would provide yet another important political signal of eu support in the current difficult circumstances. the commission and the eeas’ services, together with the member states, are also exploring a possible new sanctions regime targeting individuals who seek to destabilise this country. despite all the challenges that moldova is facing, in june, it will host the second summit of the european political community. i want to reassure you that the eu stands in solidarity with the people of moldova, even in these challenging times and the future of moldova and its citizens is within the european union.
on behalf of the epp group. mr president, commissioner, colleagues, in order to live in safety and security within the borders of the european union, it is essential that we are surrounded in our immediate neighbourhood by states that are safe and stable. the republic of moldova is a neighbour of the european union and the republic of moldova is a neighbour of ukraine. therefore, a pro-european republic of moldova is good for the citizens of the republic of moldova, but it is also important for the european union, and for ukraine. we know that the citizens of the republic of moldova want european integration, we know that the current authorities in chisinau, the presidency, the government, the parliamentary majority, want european integration, and we know who does not want it: the russian federation and the oligarchs who have been accustomed to controlling the business environment, the political scene and the media in the republic of moldova in recent years. so what do we do? we must continue to help the republic of moldova. first, we need to help the republic of moldova financially so that the authorities can help the citizens of the republic of moldova and the businesses of the republic of moldova to get through this period. we have to tell the republic of moldova, after the implementation of the nine reforms we have requested, what's next? and i say: as soon as these new reforms are implemented, we must start negotiations for accession to the european union this year, we must liberalize trade with the republic of moldova, so that the republic of moldova, like ukraine, can export as much as possible to us, can develop economically and even before accession becomes possible, because accession is a process that will take several years, we must integrate the republic of moldova into the european internal market. this will bring benefits to the people. everything we do for ukraine we must do for the republic of moldova in the next period.
on behalf of the s&d group. – mr president, the european parliament will remain a strong supporter of all of the reform processes in moldova. with a change of political context following the russian invasion of ukraine and pro-european orientation of the country, moldovan efforts should be acknowledged in many ways. we should do more. we cannot forget that part of moldovan territory has been under russian occupation for a long time. citizens of moldova have been enduring this kremlin—fuelled separatism which has cost them dearly. it would be irresponsible and naive to think that russia has now focused exclusively on ukraine. on the contrary, we need to be very careful in our immediate neighbourhood. that is why it is highly disturbing to witness these attempts to destabilise political leadership in the country: a well—known scenario set by corrupt pro—kremlin oligarchs and their parties seeks to misuse the dire economic situation to divert the country from its european union path. it’s a hybrid warfare of cynicism developed by the kremlin to destroy our european house, our values and our democracy. the defenders of democracy and our values are nowadays our neighbours, and we should not overlook this, but give them strong support.
on behalf of the renew group. mr president, commissioner, colleagues, president maia sandu and the people of moldova have chosen a path. they chose a model of society built on democratic rules, on individual rights and freedoms, on an open economy. this model is in stark contrast to putin's vision of how a country works. in putin's world, there are the privileged and the subservient, there is brute force as the only source of truth and legitimacy. in putin's world, oligarchs are given access to resources, tolerated stealing and enriching themselves at the expense of the rest of society in exchange for blind loyalty. the success of the model chosen by the republic of moldova and president sandu or the success of the model chosen by ukraine and president zelenskiy only negates the model of putin. that's what the war in ukraine is about and that's what the constant hybrid attack on the republic of moldova and its political leadership is about. putin-affiliated moldovan oligarchs are now leading this attack, paying protest mercenaries, manipulating the opinions of the vulnerable, and all to sabotage democracy and the european path for which moldovans are making so many sacrifices. we, the european parliament, have to make sure that we continue to support moldova and if mr borrell were here, i would ask him to add the moldovan oligarchs, shor, platon, plahotniuc to the eu sanctions list.
on behalf of the ecr group. – mr president, commissioner, despite difficulties, provocations, challenges, the hybrid warfare parts of the toolbox – as mentioned before – are already implemented by the russian federation on the territory of moldova, the country, led by courageous president maia sandu, keeps firm on the european and transatlantic path. we have to stand by moldovan society and also by moldovan authorities, who are our neighbours and who try everything possible to come closer to eu membership. we have to support them and open negotiations as soon as possible, possibly this year. i would like us to implement all financial and technical measures necessary to assist this country, in particular to send a civilian csdp mission there to assist moldova.
on behalf of the band the left. mr president, it is clear that the european parliament must give its full support to moldova, which is facing the concrete consequences of the war in ukraine, which are first and foremost humanitarian. the moldovan people have had to take in tens of thousands of refugees. the political consequences too, as has been said, of the unrest, the more or less manipulated demonstrations, the attempts at destabilisation, the events that are multiplying, the threatening words of mr lavrov, who saw in moldova a new ukraine, the declaration of independence of tiraspol. in short, we know these difficulties. and then there is a severe economic crisis, with a very large trade deficit and a purchasing power that is completely overwhelmed by the energy bill. so we must reiterate our support, of course, for the territorial integrity of moldova. we also need to strengthen our macrofinancial assistance. i think that although there are subsidies, i think there are still too many loans. and when we see the situation facing the moldovan people, i think we should not be complacent. we must give full and complete support to a country facing a very serious crisis.
mr president, commissioner, i would like to thank you for your reply. in recent months, the republic of moldova has increasingly become a target of russian influence. the economic situation is tense, the security environment is fragile. moscow is restricting energy supplies and trying to destabilize the pro-european government through orchestrated protests. the aim is clear and has already been mentioned by many speakers. the reform-oriented anti-corruption and pro-eu course is to be undermined. despite these adverse circumstances, the government of president maia sandu has succeeded in pushing forward an ambitious reform programme. the candidate status granted in june 2022 is our promise to the republic of moldova's long-term european perspective. the european union must and will continue to support the government in chisinau with determination and consistency in this difficult situation. it is a question of stabilizing the structures of the state. it's about improving the economic environment, and it's about building resilience to russian disinformation. in this context, ladies and gentlemen, i expressly support the plans to send a civilian csdp mission to the country as soon as possible to strengthen the moldovan crisis management structures. the next meeting of the european political community will take place in chişinău on 1 june. this will be an important occasion to show the republic of moldova the support of all european partners.
mr. president, the situation in the republic of moldova is worrying. i saw the imprint of evil recently when i was in chisinau, and i see it more and more often on the news. our partner urgently needs substantial support from brussels. the seeds of conflict are being sown in moldova in the well-known pattern: destabilization is intensifying, pro-russian groups are reactivating, the staging in transnistria is becoming more and more subtle. the kremlin is in a hurry. we have to be faster than him. but moldova also needs help to find its internal political balance. respect for european values does not and cannot have a political colour. in crucial moments, when you need all the allies, dividing, unmeritedly blaming and ostracizing a portion of the genuinely pro-european electorate just because it comes from the opposition, from the left of the political spectrum, is counterproductive and can become destructive. only by keeping your friends close can you make your enemies irrelevant. for moldova, the solidarity of the european union, energy, money, reforms and tolerance are vital now. together they will ensure the survival of democracy and the european integration it seeks.
mr president, ladies and gentlemen, you are politicians, some of you have held government posts in your own state, and you know the challenges that exist even in peacetime and in prosperous countries. now imagine that you're running a small country politically, that has been under russian influence for decades, that has a border with war-torn ukraine and russian soldiers illegally on its territory. that's why i would like to hear vice president borrell today, congratulating with us both president maia sandu and the government of the republic of moldova, for facing this pressure with immense challenges from putin's russia, but also to understand that moldova now needs the support of the european union in deeds, not just in statements. economic support, financial support, to be able to demonstrate to the citizens of the republic of moldova that a democratic government, a government supported by the european union can also cope with the economic situation and crises. for putin's challenges to fail, the european union must help moldova cope with the crises it faces, both in terms of security and in terms of the economy and the livelihoods of moldovan citizens.
mr president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, the republic of moldova is still a victim of the arbitrary ribbentrop-molotov pact, which led to the division of europe in 1939 between hitler and stalin. following this pact, stalin occupied bessarabia from romania, which he incorporated into the soviet union under the name of the moldavian soviet republic. russia, whether it was tsarist, soviet or post-soviet, did everything possible to keep the territory of the republic of moldova under its own malign sphere of influence. in addition to maintaining the frozen conflict in transnistria, russia is today undermining moldova economically, politically and culturally. the republic of moldova must be helped by all means to get out of the clutches of imperialist russia, which for hundreds of years has been seeking to achieve slavery and poverty. moldova has recently been granted eu candidate status, but this needs to be complemented by effective security support to moldova so that the front in ukraine does not extend to chisinau. it is also imperative that the european union sign a roaming agreement with moldova as a matter of urgency. such a roaming agreement is not a big effort for the eu, but it will mean everything for the citizens of the republic of moldova, who will be able to communicate much easier with their brothers and families in the european union.
mr president, ladies and gentlemen, we are facing a tense situation in the republic of moldova, right on the borders of the european union, commissioner, where attempts are being made to destabilise the country shortly after the moldovans chose a european democratic path and the european union offered moldova the status of a candidate country. modern means, such as the internet and freedom of speech, are being abused by pro-russian forces, who want to bring this state back under the influence of russia and putin, re-establish an authoritarian regime. the republic of moldova needs our support, both declaratively but especially practically, given its limited defense capabilities, limited experience on the democratic path and an already badly tried economy, the pandemic, the war in ukraine, energy dependence, the separatist zone of transnistria. we cannot stand by and watch russia and putin try to destabilize european democracies one by one, spreading disinformation and manipulation. we cannot allow uncertainty to spread across our continent.
mr president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, recently at the munich security conference, president maia sandu said that the greatest challenge for the republic of moldova is the propaganda of the russian federation, which it is applying against the republic of moldova. i ask you, commissioner, what is the commission's strategy to support the republic of moldova in the face of these extremely aggressive instruments that the kremlin is using today on the ground, when we see that, with great aggressiveness, through propaganda, manipulation, disinformation, the republic of moldova is trying to be destabilised by those who have joined hands, and here i am referring to corrupt politicians, to pro-russian political forces, to fugitives who are no longer in the republic of moldova, but who are financing entire campaigns to denigrate and destabilise the pro-european government in chisinau. we are waiting for action because solidarity is needed now more than ever.
madam president, of course improving the energy performance of buildings is something we all want to achieve, especially in the face of today's environmental challenges. of course we all want lower bills and more efficient spending, but first of all let's ask ourselves if the targets we are setting are realistic, if they meet the expectations of european citizens, if the transition to zero-emission buildings from 2030 is smooth enough that it does not affect some countries and many end-users. i certainly support the green transition, that it is a necessity, but not before we make sure that it is viable and will not affect vulnerable citizens, who are already heavily affected by all the measures of the last months and years.
madam president, i voted against the energy performance of buildings report because it will lead to a severe increase in home rental prices, which will make the very few rich people richer and the rest of the people poorer. every time a european bureaucrat proposes something and labels it as ‘ambitious’, that means it is actually bad for the people, and this report just proves that. it is absurd to have the so-called ambitions that are converting into more obligations for the people which are costing them even more money. for example, the member states will have to ensure that the use of fossil fuel heating systems will not be authorised in existing buildings undergoing major renovations, deep renovation or renovation of heating systems, as well as in new buildings, starting from 24 months after the adoption of this directive. the solution left for the people is to heat their homes using electricity, which is more and more expensive, mainly due to european green deal utopian objectives. europe needs a pragmatic leadership, clearly different than the current one, a leadership that can provide practical solutions to increase economic growth or people’s health while reducing costs and making the continent a better, safer and cleaner place to live.
madam president, technological progress has brought us face to face with new challenges and that is why i think it is essential to have clear rules governing this area. we need fairness in the digital environment, and that's why i think it's necessary that users, as well as manufacturers, can leverage the data from the devices they're connected to. these new rules that we are preparing are essential precisely because this issue is important to be able to use and access data generated in the european union in all economic sectors. this law mandates and obviously provides equity in the digital environment to stimulate a competitive data market, to open up new opportunities for data-driven innovation and will make data more accessible to all.
madam president, i voted in favour of the european ombudsman's 2021 activity report because, under the leadership of emily o'reilly, it was one of the few european institutions that really protects citizens' rights and has the courage to call out and hold the european commission and ursula von der leyen accountable for the abuses that have been committed. in 2021, emily oreilly opened numerous investigations into the lack of transparency of the european commission and other european institutions: one such investigation into how ursula von der leyen negotiated and purchased, in a totally non-transparent and illegal way, billions of doses of vaccines with public money, through text messages exchanged personally with the ceo of pfizer, albert bourla, and another investigation into the refusal of the european medicines agency to grant public access to documents related to the manufacture of mrna vaccines against covid-19. it's unfair to deny the public the right to know how the injections they were forced to take during the pandemic were tested. i commend the european ombudsman for not giving in to pressure and exercising his mandate of democratic scrutiny over european public institutions. following this report, ursula von der leyen should, if not out of honour, at least out of shame, resign.
madam president, the european union, as the world's largest donor of development assistance, stands out clearly from its other international partners. european action on the international stage is guided by key principles such as democracy, the rule of law, the universality and indivisibility of human rights and fundamental freedoms, respect for human dignity, the principle of equality and solidarity, and respect for the principles of the united nations charter and international law, which leads to the implementation of key humanitarian objectives such as leaving no one behind and addressing first the needs of the most deprived, with the ambition to reduce and eradicate poverty in the long term. the fact that the european parliament voted by a very large majority on this report reaffirms the european union's foreign policy mandate to contribute to a fairer world and to well-being for all. we have a strong signal for the footprint the european union wants to leave on the world.
madam president, your excellences, do not turn the drama of the romanian farmers into a second schengen unfairly denied to romania! the 23-24 march council is obliged to decide on the payment of compensation to farmers who have lost enormously after the launch of the russian invasion of ukraine. romanian producers have calculated a loss of over eur 200 million, but there are also poland, slovakia, hungary, the czech republic and bulgaria. the union's assistance to ukraine since the beginning of the war must continue. we must continue to support even more the ukrainians' struggle to take their country out from under the threat of a tyrant. they die to protect us, and if ukraine falls, we're not safe. but the union cannot leave any of its citizens in the lurch, including romanian farmers! you have to find a solution! romanian farmers expect it and have a right to it. and, after all, does not competitiveness mean quality cereals in europe? it's the same romanians who were so offended when they were denied full access to schengen. i pointed out that if the european institutions feed the discontent of the population with wrong decisions, the wave of euroscepticism will increase and the extremists will exult. eight years ago, romanians were the most euro-optimistic, 68 percent, when the european average was 54. now, after the completely unfair decision to expand schengen in december, they have become the most euro-pessimistic, 46%, compared to the european average, which is 62%. it's a trend that started during the pandemic, when romania was the playground of russian disinformation and exploded post the schengen decision in december. your excellences, do not abandon romania! it is not possible! we are a key state for the defense of the nato and eu border!
i have to say, madam president, the competition for green jobs is heightened. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the union's energy market is not affected by the price pressure of the us dollar. the eu's green jersey has been challenged from both the east and the west. both because more countries have become aware of the climate challenges we face. this is good, but also because it has actually become a good business case, and therefore the eu is naturally forced to react when the united states pulls out the checkbook and encourages european companies to move their production to the united states. anything else would be far from reality. but we must do so in a way that does not close in on ourselves and in which the investments we make are both temporary and targeted. no one benefits from a state aid axis, not even the eu. and the world has changed. we have to face that before it's too late. so dear germany and dear epp: this also applies to the phase-out of combustion engines in cars. the future is green, even when it comes to our cars.
madam president, ladies and gentlemen, last july matteo salvini, the current italian government minister, said that the left would be pushed by putin. today, however, it is precisely the right-wing government ministers who point to wagner, putin's mercenary militias, who have been responsible for horrific crimes in ukraine, as responsible for the recent arrivals. we are at the comic theatre, salvini said. maybe he saw the future. human beings are dying on our shores, blamed for the indecent words of minister piantedosi, who calls irresponsible parents those who flee from despair. what can i say? i'm ashamed of him. but now we need policy, the good policy, the policy that changes things. the european council must act by changing the pact on migration through legal and safe channels, with a new european system for rescue and search at sea. let's just be human.
madam president, i am grateful that we are talking about the economy after some time because it is a very important topic, so let me make two takes. the first, i don’t believe that the economy should be run by subsidies or by state aid. this should be used very carefully and we are in stage that we are considering we should not forget that the driving force should be competition and competitiveness, together with transparent and simple rules. the second, we should not forget when comparing with the us that we pay a high cost for our policy being fragmented. where the us is taking one policy that is valid across the eu, we have very often 20 different fragmented policies. the second points i want to remind us that we are struggling with very high inflation, and high inflation is boosted by expansionary fiscal policy. we should more discuss about correction of the fiscal policy and about introduction of much more better fiscal rules across the eu.
madam president, dear vice-president, mr president of the european council, mrs monserrat, i know that the opposition is very cold, but i do not think that lies will contribute much to today's debate. that said, ladies and gentlemen, although we are coming to the end of winter, many families and businesses, and especially smes, are still suffering the consequences of the crisis caused by putin's war. although we have been able to avoid energy shortages this winter, poverty and energy insecurity are still a reality that we have to overcome. we need a european energy policy now, in the medium and long term, which promotes the geopolitical diversification of the supply of clean and affordable energy. one of the keys to this lies in the reform of the european electricity market. i therefore hope that the proposals of the spanish vice-president, teresa ribera, will be put to good use under the spanish presidency, and i have the support of the european people's party. on the other hand, we need to deploy renewable energy and green hydrogen, key elements to achieve a just ecological transition that both humanity and the planet need. ladies and gentlemen, good times are coming. let us take the opportunity to consolidate a genuine common energy policy.
madam president, this mandate thus far has been marked by our commitment to climate change, which is good. so we have net zero emissions, we have fit for 55 with the green deal, etc. but that also has led to headlines across europe saying the eu is forcing us to do this and forcing us to do that. now, i would like to see the rest of this mandate being dedicated to highlighting the supports we can give to member states and businesses in dealing with this challenge so that the headlines would be the eu is helping us to reach our climate goals and at the same time maintaining or improving our competitiveness. that is absolutely vital from now on to change the narrative. and also, of course, i am wondering what has happened to investeu. in the last mandate known as the juncker fund, it was hugely successful in generating finance and jobs, etc. we hear nothing about it now. we need to hear more about these instruments so people can see that the eu is supporting them in reaching the climate change goals.
madam president, it’s been very interesting. i’ve been listening to the discussion and i have several questions. i would have wanted to ask the epp about the new trade agreement with the us. i would have wanted to ask the council about the potential ice ban. i want to talk about the greens with their naive view on mining activities and to the left on the electricity market reform. but we had no blue card, so there is no possibility to have a debate. and the result is we have more visitors here than meps and we have become completely irrelevant. we’re going and moving towards elections. we need to have more lively debate and discussions. come on, we can do better in this house. so, in the future, i hope we can have more lively debates and make us more relevant because we earn that, the citizens earn that and we can do better than this, colleagues.
ms wiesner, this is precisely why you had the one minute: to use the catch-the-eye in order to ask a question to the commission or the council presidents.
i am not saying that we should not be concerned about the situation in the former yugoslavia. i am naturally pleased that the inflation reduction act now includes european-produced raw materials. but i'm really worried that we're losing our partnerships in the global south like this. we are faced with the huge challenge of diversifying supply chains. that means that we must first of all make ourselves independent of china. the partner countries in east africa - whether it is the congo, uganda or tanzania - want manufacturing industries themselves. let's invest in it. let us give these countries the chance to grow, the chance to really contribute to the prosperity of their own people. that's my big request to you. i would like to ask the commissioner whether he is aware of the fact that the commission has not yet taken a decision on the matter.

vice-president of the commission. – madam president, president of the european council, honourable members, i think that this debate has very clearly proven that the race for global leadership in net—zero clean tech industry is very much on. i agree with mr lamberts that the european economy is, and will always be, based on ‘high value added’. therefore, we have developed the concept of competitive sustainability, and i really would like to thank this house for the support for the battery regulation, where we place this concept for the first time into full practice. each battery placed on the eu market will have a qr code telling us exactly what is the carbon footprint, where the critical raw materials came from, whether they have been ethically sourced and, of course, what the warranty is for their recycling. i think that thanks to this approach we have over the last five years got more than eur 180 billion of investment into this sector with 160 industrial projects and more than 30 gigafactories, which are being built in europe despite all global competition. of course, i totally agree with all of you that these investments must continue. so i would like to also reassure ms charanzová that we are in close contact with the industry, and the proposals you heard this morning from the president of the european commission on fast permitting, on granting better access to public and private finance, and to have a forward—looking, critical raw materials act, as mr gallée was suggesting, to make sure that we will use the new approach to state aid and work with green procurement, as ms cavazzini was referring to, and to develop the concept of how to use tax breaks and simplify our procedures, are exactly the responses the industry was expecting from us. so what i want to say is that this is what was proposed by our industrial leaders and this is what we are going to deliver. i believe that this is a powerful signal that we want european industry not only to stay in europe, but to expand and prosper here as well. ms garcía pérez, mr moreno sánchez and mr schirdewan have been making very important remarks, together with several honourable members, on how the high energy prices are affecting our households and our industry. yesterday’s reform, presented by my colleague kadri simson on the eu electricity market, should not only accelerate the deployment of renewables, but also protect our households and our industry from unreliable suppliers, from hikes in electricity pricing, and help us to overcome the dependencies which have been proven to be so dangerous for our security of supply. as you know, our leaders in the european council have decided that we should, for the first time in the history of the european union, do our utmost and to purchase gas in a common way. because we know that gas will play the role of a necessary transitional fuel for some time. we have talked to major gas consumers, to steelers, to fertiliser producers, to aluminium smelters and operators, to smes in the textile, glass and ceramic industry, and they’ve been very obliged to us that we are actually aggregating their demands and that we will enter into the negotiations with the global suppliers, because otherwise they will simply not be able to reach that level of the negotiation. so what we are looking for here is to get the best prices and the best conditions from global markets for economy in the european union. yesterday, we opened the process of registration for all those who want to purchase gas together, and in may, as i said, for the first time in history, we will start joint procurement of gas for eu consumers. we believe that the common political and economic weight of the european economy will bring us not only better security of supply, but also lower energy prices, which are so crucial for addressing the cost of living of our families, but also the competitiveness of our industry. to conclude, i really would like to thank this house for the strong and continued support for ukraine, which is as needed now as it was a year ago.
the european council madam president, ladies and gentlemen, thank you for your messages. i would like to comment on some of the points that have been raised. first, as many have said, we are facing a major challenge. after the brexit upheaval, after the covid, after the awareness of the need to be engaged in the climate field, in the digital field, we are facing a war on the european continent. for the past year, the unity of the member states in decision-making has been unfailing in supporting ukraine, which is fighting for its future, which is also fighting for our values, which is fighting for our democratic principles. we had the opportunity to take additional, operational steps, because that meeting a few weeks ago with president zelensky, in a small format, was the opportunity, in an operational way, to consider additional supports in military capacity to really help and support ukraine. and this war against ukraine is also an opportunity to open our eyes to the need to take steps forward for a europe of defence. secondly, we are also united in sanctioning russia, in trying to isolate it internationally. it is the diplomatic battle that is being fought and we realise that it is not simple. it is necessary to engage in a body of diplomatic work, to speak to the entire international community, to argue and defend these principles which directly affect a rules-based international order. and then many, each in his own way, each with his own political sensibilities, have made it clear that our generation, whatever the bench on which we stand, is facing a major challenge. after this political project was set up on the ashes of two tragic wars, our generation must now make decisions that will have an impact on how the european union will be shaped in the coming decades for our children and for our grandchildren. it is about strengthening the resilience, the resilience, the solidity, the robustness of this european project. i see four key elements, and several have mentioned them directly or indirectly. the first is to work again and again to support a strong and robust economic base which is the condition for leaving no one behind, to ensure that there are transitions that are fair, just. but the basic condition is the ability to turn, as has been said, to innovation, to investment, to fight against bureaucracy, against red tape, to ensure that there is a capacity to free up the minds, to free up the entrepreneurial energies to build and shape a europe that looks to the future with more confidence and more solidity. the second point, and we have been at the heart of this political and economic battle for the last few months, is energy. and we don't find out with the war in ukraine, we knew that long before. and long before that, we were very divided among europeans on how to approach this question of our energy future. there were sometimes heated political debates between us on the subject. it is striking to me to see in a few months the leap forward in political convergence towards greater europeanisation of the energy question. this is exactly the work that is being done with the european council and the commission. the european parliament has its say on the subject. who would have imagined two or three years ago that today we would be talking about a common purchasing platform for energy? who would have thought that we would be in a moment when we would accelerate this ambition to develop renewable energy for more independence, for more sovereignty, and that we would have these debates on the different technologies that are needed in order to have, tomorrow, the capacities that are useful to protect competitiveness, to protect prosperity and our ability to support families all over europe? firstly, the economic basis. secondly, the energy question. and third, safety. security and defense. and here again, i say it again, but this project which is now becoming operational and real - mobilizing the european industry to set up joint purchases of ammunition - is an extremely important step. we will be working a lot on this with the high representative josep borrell, with the member states. the matter will be on the table again at the european council next week. we really need to take more responsibility for our security and stability. the fourth point, last but not least, is at the heart of our dna. it is at the heart of who we are as europeans, because our reality has been forged in the course of a history that has been tragic, that has been brutal in the last century. it is the dignity of every human being, it is the fundamental freedoms. it is the idea that we have a special responsibility to take this conviction of genuine dignity around the world. i am very proud, as a european representative, with all the member states, that the european union is by far the first sponsor of multilateralism, the first sponsor of efforts within the framework of the united nations, for example, to reduce discrimination, injustice and impoverishment. and i am very proud that this issue of equality between women and men is at the heart of our project. there's a very important ceremony coming up in a few moments. the council stands firmly with the women in iran and afghanistan who are courageously standing up for their dignity. finally, there is a crucial point, namely the question of europe's place in the world. that's what some of you have said. the question for the next few decades is: do we want to be content with a european union where decisions are made by others, or do we want to build a powerful europe in which we are masters of our own destiny and our own future? this is the question that is being asked in the geopolitical debate that we are facing today. you understand the choice i'm making. i think our responsibility, through strengthening the economic and labour base for our security, is to ensure that we do not delegate to others the conditions of our future, but rather that we decide as europeans, in the democratic debate among ourselves, how we create the legitimacy to take today the decisions that will shape the project in which our children and grandchildren will live tomorrow. a project of peace, prosperity, stability based on those fundamental values to which we are so attached.
the debate is closed. written statements (rule 171)
in writing. while states and peoples are faced with economic and social difficulties which are far from inevitable, the commission and the council are working together to deepen a framework of constraints which are almost absolute in relation to the action of states and which limits the necessary measures of public investment and social progress which counteract the consequences of the policies which you are putting in place. i am referring in particular to the full reintroduction of the rules of the stability pact and the so-called reform of macroeconomic governance. without changing the absurd rules of deficit and debt in the main, they are moving forward with the creation of new and faster mechanisms for blackmailing, monitoring, controlling and sanctioning states, trying to ensure that, as merkel said, governments change but not policies. we know the objectives and consequences, which affect mainly countries like portugal: wage and pension restraint, divestment in public services, labour deregulation, liberalisation of strategic sectors... the way forward is to abolish the macroeconomic governance framework and the constraints associated with it.
in writing. the russian terrorist regime has been committing war crimes for over a year, and the european union must not relent in its aid to the ukrainian people, whether humanitarian, economic or military. on the contrary, we must increase aid. i stress that the ukrainians are not only fighting for their freedom, but for the freedom of all democratic europe, and we must provide them with everything they need for this defence. i therefore call for a common european procedure for the procurement of ammunition and for the joint purchase and supply of artillery ammunition to ukraine. if approved and implemented, this will be another positive step in helping ukraine and building a common and more effective european defence.
the extraordinary european council of 9 february, which included an exchange of views with president zelensky, reaffirmed the union's commitment to systemic support for ukraine, recognising its right to independence, sovereignty and territorial integrity. it was also decided to move forward with the structural measures of the zero impact industrial plan, in particular with regard to the flexibility of state aid, financing, the regulatory framework, capacity building, capital markets and multilateral trade and cooperation agreements. the council adopted a set of guidelines to regulate migration flows in a transparent, inclusive and humanistic manner. the conclusions listed are promising but require rapid and effective implementation. the european council of 23 and 24 march will follow up this and adopt the principles of energy market reform. at a critical moment in responding to the disruption of value chains and rising prices, with the purchasing power of large sections of the european population being undermined, the european union cannot fall back into the trap of austerity policies whose economic and financial ineffectiveness and social cost were demonstrated in the subprime crisis.
in relation to the decision by several committees to enter into interinstitutional negotiations, pursuant to rule 71(1), announced at the opening of the sitting on monday 13 march, i have received requests for a vote in parliament pursuant to rule 71(2) from members reaching at least the medium threshold on the decision to enter into negotiations on the european digital identity framework. the vote will be held tomorrow, 16 march 2023. regarding the other decisions, i have received no request for a vote and, therefore, the committees may start the negotiations. we move to the next item of this morning, an important one. i am very pleased, dear members and dear colleagues, to welcome you at this year’s celebration of international women’s day. we have a packed and very well prepared programme and i would like to ask you dear colleagues to welcome a number of special guests that we have brought together. before that we will start with a round of political group speakers.
president of the group of the progressive alliance of socialists and democrats. madam president, the truth is that it is an honour for all of us to have two women here today who are leaders in their respective fields. we need women who inspire a new generation of girls and teenagers who see their potential reflected in the achievements of women like shirin ebadi, the first muslim woman to win the nobel peace prize. mrs ebadi was the first iranian woman to become president of a court in 1975 and, despite the many problems and obstacles that were put in her way, she has never given up and has continued to fight. she was able to practice as a lawyer and focused on defending the weakest, political prisoners and, especially, women and children. women like samantha cristoforetti, the third european woman astronaut to reach space and the second woman in the world with the most days in space. there have been very few female astronauts, but thanks to the example of mrs. cristoforetti, many girls will realize that it is possible to break through into a man's world and break through the glass ceiling. but today, you'll allow me to end with a tribute to the millions of anonymous women who have made history. mothers who return home after long days of work and get down to doing homework with their children and housework. the rural women who get up every morning to tend the livestock, work hours and hours and never get recognized. to the women who have been victims of gender-based violence, many of whom lose their lives while others have to make a new one. and to the women who are on behalf of the renew group. madam president, simone de beauvoir said that a political, economic or religious crisis was enough to call women's rights into question again and that these rights could never be taken for granted. unfortunately, he was right. today we are aware of it: in so many places, in iran, afghanistan, the united states, and here, within our borders, in poland, where yesterday a woman activist was convicted precisely for helping others exercise their right to abortion. it is an honour to have people like shirin ebadi, who today reminds us again in this house of the cry of women, life and freedom. the cry of millions of women against a regime that has murdered thousands of women and poisoned 7,000 students and is the first country in the world to execute women. we must remind afghan women that they live in a true gender apartheid and we have to do a lot for them. above all, not to recognize the criminal regimes that oppress them. it's also an honor to be able to be with women like samantha, because education is the most powerful engine for equality. in an industry where 90 percent of astronauts are men, she's changing the narrative of stereotypes, of gender bias, and she's sending a message to girls: you can be anything you want, you can fulfill all your dreams. but it's also a reminder to us, to know that we have to keep working every day so that all women have access to all rights. you can't ask less of a democracy because women can't expect less. that is why we must continue to work on the directive on combating gender-based violence, which comes late, when 50 women are killed, victims of violence in the european union, and on equal pay, equal representation. today is a day to remember that no right to equality can be taken for granted.
co-president of the group of the greens/european free alliance. – madam president, dear colleagues, today we are celebrating women. we are celebrating women in the fight for their rights. we are celebrating women in their fight against patriarchy. but let me say this: women are for long already not only carrying the fight for women’s rights. they are carrying across the globe the fight for democracy and freedom – values that the european union is founded on. if we look at iran, if we look at afghanistan, if we look at argentina, there are brave women who are fighting for what we hold dear and they are a source of inspiration to all of us. one of the women that we should be celebrating today, and for international women’s day, is justyna wydrzyńska, not only because she has shown bravery and commitment, but because she has helped others. she has helped people in need. she supported women who needed access to abortion in a country like poland, where the right and the access to abortion has been heavily restricted over the past years. this form of compassion and solidarity is what makes our society strong. she has stood up for our values and very concretely provided support. but instead of celebrating her, she is being criminalised. she was convicted to eight months of community service yesterday for caring for others, for defending our rights. colleagues, we cannot stay silent on this – full solidarity to justyna and all the women in poland and elsewhere in the world who are standing up for their rights, who are standing up for care and solidarity. i do not want to live in a europe where women who are helping other women are being criminalised. so let us take this day and let us take action together. let us put the right to abortion into the eu charter on fundamental rights. women in europe deserve it. let us be a beacon of hope and inspiration for all women across the globe.
