president-in-office of the council. – mr president, honourable members, mr vice-president, from what i hear today, it is clear that the expectations are high and that is good. clearly, there are many opinions on this topic, which is also good. it shows the significance of these questions after our discussion here today. i remain – as the vice-president also said – confident that we will reach a compromise within the set time frame together. the march council stated a view to conclude the legislative work in 2023. yes, this is a tight time frame, but it is not an impossible time frame. as i said in my first statement, we need an updated framework in place to make sure that we have rules to support sound public finance, the reforms that promote growth and that keep our union strong and competitive. and i would like to reassure you that the presidency is committed to work hard to advance as far as possible during this last leg of our presidency, and that we are also in close contact with the incoming spanish presidency to ensure a smooth transition at the end of june. and we can count on the constructive spirit of all stakeholders involved to move this forward swiftly. and from what i hear here today in the debate, that is also a common view.
the debate is closed. written declarations (article 171) the following documents shall be submitted to the court of justice:
in writing. – the stability and growth pact defines the direction to be followed in the fiscal policies of eurozone member states. in the absence of a federalising budget, sgp rules have functioned as a sub-optimal mechanism for the eurozone to manage financial stability with patchy effectiveness. for political reasons, deficit rules have not been applied equally to all member states. states in fiscal surplus declined to launch expansionary policies so as to promote macroeconomic balance across the eurozone. the rules have served to restrict public investment as needed budgetary cuts largely targeted investment envelopes. the updates now being proposed were meant to correct these problems. proposals are actually at the lowest common denominator of what could have been expected. the dilemmas encountered in past policymaking will persist. so will the political imbalance that swayed past spg-related decisions. more sustainable and bolder rule changes would have been appropriate, like increased flexibility allowing for fiscal adjustments to carry higher levels of public investment, plus a stronger focus on the social dimension. admittedly, the difficulties in securing consensus for a more comprehensive approach are significant. i disagree with the establishment of a federalising eurozone budget. consequently, i will support the proposals made, with amendments, as they are better than no change.
dear colleagues, on europe day – this day of symbolism, of history and of the future – we welcome germany’s bundeskanzler, olaf scholz. herr bundeskanzler, herzlich willkommen im europaparlament. ‘world peace cannot be safeguarded without the making of creative efforts proportionate to the dangers which threaten it’ – so begins the declaration presented by robert schuman on 9 may 1950. it rings true today. every year, on this day, we celebrate europe – an unprecedented project of reconciliation fundamentally based on solidarity, a project that brings people together without trying to make us all the same, a project that lit a light that permeated through iron curtains and concrete walls. europe day reminds us of what is possible when we come together, of the responsibility we have to keep moving forward. the schuman declaration, our european union, took bravery. change takes courage. the european union is not perfect, and i know many share our frustrations with some of our processes. but the fundamental pillars of hope, of possibility, of freedom, democracy and the rule of law, make this political project unique. we cannot take what we stand for, what we have achieved – and what we must achieve still – for granted. we must keep evolving. european progress was made possible thanks to daring solutions. and more daring solutions will be needed going forward. i know that we can count on germany for just that. yours, dear chancellor, is a member state which shows unwavering commitment to the betterment of europe. so let me thank you for germany’s support for ukraine; for germany’s contribution to the construction of a new eu security architecture; for germany pushing new technologies; for germany defending human rights, such as the rights women and men in iran; and for so much more. chancellor you once said ‘we have every reason to be more optimistic about our future’. that is the spirit that must drive us forward. we must reform, anticipate change – not suffer it. we must find that courage that underpinned the schuman declaration again. we must help that light keep shining brighter. we know that we are so much stronger, when we are together, and we will look to germany – as to all members states – to help reform and prepare our european future. the european union matters. it is worth it. es lebe europa!
the next item is ‘this is europe – debate with the chancellor of germany, olaf scholz’ (2023/2665(rsp)). herr bundeskanzler, the floor is yours.
federal chancellor of the federal republic of germany. the commission has already made a number of proposals to this effect. i would like to thank you for the opportunity to speak to you today, on europe day, in this special place. i am honoured and touched by your invitation. it is an honour to be here, because as freely elected members of parliament you represent 450 million europeans - and therefore the citizens of europe. and it touches me because may 9th is the only correct, forward-looking answer to the world war unleashed by germany, to destructive nationalism and imperialist megalomania. 73 years ago today, french foreign minister robert schuman proposed the creation of a new 'organised and vibrant europe'. the community's activities began with the pooling of coal and steel, the commodities which for decades had been used to produce arms. weapons our grandfathers and great-grandfathers used to shoot each other. the dream of the fathers and mothers of europe was to put an end to this mutual killing once and for all. that dream has come true for us. war between our peoples has become unthinkable the european union to the thanks and happiness of us all. but a look beyond the immediate neighbourhood of our union shows in a terrible way that this dream is not a reality in all the countries of europe. the people of ukraine are defending their freedom and democracy, their sovereignty and independence against a brutal russian invasion army, day after day, at great cost. we support them in this. the founding fathers and mothers of the european union gave the unifying europe a task which went far beyond its internal pacification. it was clear to them that europe had a global responsibility, because the well-being of europe was inseparable from the well-being of the rest of the world! the schuman declaration reads as follows: this production means coal and steel will be made available to the whole world without distinction or exception, in order to contribute to raising the standard of living and to promoting the works of peace. europe will then be able to devote more resources to one of its most important tasks: the development of the african continent. this 'development of the african continent' was then mainly countered by the european colonial exploitation of our neighbouring continent. this is why tackling the consequences of colonialism must be the essence of any european partnership with the countries of africa, asia and latin america, a partnership that leaves behind the eurocentric outlook of the past decades, a partnership that not only maintains but establishes a common ground. i believe that building such partnerships is more important than ever. the european union is a single market, and it is not just a single market. that's just five percent of the world's population. in asia, africa and south america, new economic, demographic and political heavyweights are emerging - a success, by the way, of the division of labour between countries and continents, which has lifted a billion people out of poverty. they will not tolerate a bi- or tri-polar world order, and rightly so. that 's why i 'm convinced: the world of the 21st century will be multipolar - it already is. but what does this mean for us in europe? is europe becoming, to quote the french writer paul valéry, what it really is: a small foothill, a cape on the asian mainland? we can't answer that by looking back. the european parliament has been a great help to the people of europe in the past, and it is a great help to the people of europe today. and those who constantly warn of europe's decline are not winning the future, especially since they completely underestimate one thing: how capable europe is of change and action. we have seen this time and again in the crises of the past and the present. let us remember how we came together, in solidarity and unity with partners around the world, through the last winter. the three lessons are these: firstly, the future of europe is in our hands. secondly, the more we build europe together, the easier it will be to secure a good future for ourselves. and thirdly, not less, but more openness, more cooperation is the demand of our time. the european union must be able to play a leading role in the world of tomorrow, not above and below other countries and regions, but on an equal footing with them, alongside them. the european union must change to do this. we need a geopolitical european union, an enlarged and reformed european union and, last but not least, a european union open to the future. in all this i see the european parliament as a driving force and an ally. let us take the creation of a geopolitical europe. here, in front of the european parliament, willy brandt already testified to its existential necessity 50 years ago. 'the unification of europe', he wrote in our family register at the time, 'is not just a question of the quality of our lives. it is a question of survival between the giants and in the divided world of young and old nationalisms. other the european parliament has always acted in accordance with this maxim, and i am very grateful to you for that. you will do so by upholding the rule of law and by constantly reminding us all that europe is only heard when it speaks with one voice. the recent brutal war of aggression by russia against ukraine has shown us all how indispensable this realization is. and consequently, the european union has rarely been more united than after this infamous break in the european and international peace order. this experience is the basis for a geopolitical europe. i have made some suggestions on this during my visit to charles university in prague last summer. this includes a much closer integration of our defence efforts and the building of an integrated european defence economy. the european peace facility, the joint procurement of ammunition for ukraine, the closer cooperation of many of our countries in air defence, our strategic compass, close cooperation between nato and the european union - these are all good approaches which we want to deepen and accelerate. we must now set the course for the reconstruction of ukraine. yes, this requires political and financial capital and in the very long term. and at the same time, it is a great opportunity not only for ukraine, but for europe as a whole, because a prosperous, democratic, european ukraine is the clearest rejection of putin's imperial, revisionist, international law-breaking policy on our continent. europe must also survive in global competition with other major powers. the united states remains europe's most important ally. and here's the thing: the more we invest now in our security and defense -- in civilian resilience, in technological sovereignty, in reliable supply chains, in our independence in critical raw materials -- the better allies we are to our transatlantic friends. our relationship with china has been aptly described as a triad of 'partner, competitor, systemic rival' with rivalry and competition from china having undoubtedly increased. the eu sees this and the european union is responding. i agree with ursula von der leyen that no decoupling, but clever de-risking is the motto. the countries of the global south are new partners whose concerns and legitimate interests we take seriously. that is why it is so important that europe acts in solidarity and with determination to promote food security and poverty reduction, and that we honour the commitments we have made in international climate and environmental protection. and that too is part of a geopolitical europe: it is more than reasonable that we now conclude new free trade agreements quickly with mercosur, mexico, india, indonesia, australia, kenya and, in the future, with many other countries. (applause from the centre) fair agreements that promote the economic development of our partners, not hinder it! fair, for example, means that the first processing of raw materials takes place there and not in china or elsewhere. if we incorporate this idea into our trade relations, we will also be making a major contribution to the diversification of our supply chains. europe must turn to the world. if we continue to negotiate new free trade agreements for years without success, others will dictate the rules in the future with lower environmental and social standards. we have already taken a key decision last year on the shape of a geopolitical europe. and here too the european parliament has been a driving force. we have chosen a great europe. we have said to the citizens of the western balkan countries, ukraine, moldova and, in the future, georgia: you are one of us. (applause from the centre) we want you to become part of our european union! and this is not about altruism. it's about our credibility and it's about economic sanity. and it is about securing peace in europe after the turn of events that russia's war of aggression has brought about. a geopolitical europe is also measured by whether it keeps its promises to its immediate neighbours. a genuine enlargement policy is based on its promises, first and foremost to the western balkan countries, to which we have offered accession for more than 20 years. the process of normalisation between serbia and kosovo, which has begun, and the reforms in the candidate countries must of course be continued. of course, north macedonia's political courage must be followed by rapid progress towards accession. (applause from the centre) but we must also honour such progress, otherwise enlargement policy will lose its impetus and the european union will lose its influence and radiance. the european union must be reformed. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council and parliament. and i very much welcome the fact that the european parliament is working on proposals for institutional reform, including those which do not belong to the parliament itself. i will continue to promote in the european council that we look at these ideas. the council's position is clear: more qualified majority voting in foreign policy and taxation. (applause from the centre) i will continue to work on that. and i'm very grateful for the broad support from your ranks. i would like to say to the sceptics that unanimity, not 100% agreement on all decisions, does not create the greatest possible democratic legitimacy. on the contrary! it is precisely the lobbying and the struggle for majorities and alliances that distinguish us as democrats, the search for compromises that also serve the interests of the minorities. that's exactly what we mean by liberal democracy! it is also essential for the future that we insist on respect for democratic principles and the rule of law within the eu. and here again, i know a very large majority of you are on my side. so why not use the upcoming debate on reform of the european union to strengthen the european commission's ability to launch infringement proceedings whenever our fundamental values of freedom, democracy, equality, the rule of law and respect for human rights are violated? (applause from the centre) ladies and gentlemen, there is one more element to be added - i have just mentioned it - and that is that we europeans must open ourselves to the future, without ifs and buts. first of all, it means getting rid of the old problems that have crippled us for years, that make it all too easy for other countries to divide us and play us off against each other. i am thinking, for example, of our approach to refugee migration. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council and parliament. but we cannot wait for this solidarity to come over us, as if by the holy spirit. europe, as robert schuman said 73 years ago today, is made up of 'concrete facts', of 'the solidarity of facts'. i therefore urge that the progress we have made in the reform of the common european asylum system in the council, after long and difficult negotiations, be brought to a conclusion before the european elections. their agreement last month on a negotiating position on key parts of the reform was a very important step along this path. now we must finish the work with all our might. (applause from the centre) we share the common goal of better managing and organising irregular migration without compromising our values. we can take advantage of something even more powerful than before: in many parts of europe we are now in dire need of labour, including from third countries. if we consistently link these opportunities for regular migration with the demand that countries of origin and transit take back those who have no right to stay here with us, then all sides benefit. this includes measures to ensure effective external border control, as we agreed at the european council in february. then there is growing acceptance of smart, controlled and regulated immigration in our countries. and then we strip the foundation of those who make politics out of fear and resentment. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council and parliament. i'm talking about the shift of our countries, our economies and our societies towards a climate-neutral future. the first industrial revolution began here in europe. shouldn't we be the ones to shape europe's next big change? i do not need to explain to you the opportunity this departure offers europe. it is important that the citizens of our countries also feel this in their daily lives, for example because electricity from renewable energy sources will become cheaper in the future, because there are enough charging stations for electric cars and lorries throughout europe, because new, future-oriented jobs are being created in the energy sector or in the chip industry, because here in europe we develop and market the technologies that the whole world needs for the transition towards climate neutrality. to make this change ambitious and at the same time to leave no one behind - this is the great project for the future that we europeans should be united behind now. (applause from the centre) to quote oscar wilde, "the future belongs to those who see the possibilities before they become obvious". it's not for the nostalgic. it is not the responsibility of the revisionists who dream of national glory or aspire to imperial power. (applause from the centre) the ukrainians are paying with their lives for this madness of their powerful neighbour. two hundred miles northeast of here in moscow, putin is moving his troops, tanks and missiles today. let us not be intimidated by such a power grab! let us remain steadfast in our support for ukraine as long as it takes! the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. our european union united in its diversity is the best guarantee that this past will not return. and that is why the message of 9 may is not what is coming from moscow today, but our message, and that is: the past will not triumph over the future. and the future - our future - is the european union. i am speaking on behalf of the ppe group. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council and parliament. welcome to the heart of european democracy! yesterday, may 8: we commemorate and rejoice in the liberation from the nazi state of injustice. and today, 9 may, we celebrate and commemorate the courage of the fathers and mothers of european unity. just a day of the week, it's maybe a quirk of history, a coincidence. but what is incredibly amazing is that there was only five years between these two milestones in european history. france: de gaulle, after all the german atrocities, reached out his hand to public opinion. what courage, what leadership! konrad adenauer took that hand for west germany. he even rejected stalin's proposal to accept a neutral, reunified germany. he chose european unity. he chose nato against the public opinion at the time. what courage, what leadership! yes, today we are good at management, vaccine procurement, arf, ten sanctions packages, energy crisis - all of this has been managed. but de gaulle, adenauer, kohl and mitterrand were politicians who could not only manage everyday life, but had the power to shape history. the european parliament has already spoken today, as it did in prague, of the abolition of unanimity in foreign policy, and of the building of a european defence system, including a european army, to name just two examples. and emmanuel macron has spoken like that. we have set up the conference on the future of europe specifically to discuss these issues. unfortunately, after macron's re-election, there was no initiative, there was no convention. mr chancellor, i must say this again for us: we do not need any more speeches on principles. we need the courage now to lead europe into the future. europe needs leadership! and we, as the european people's party, are now calling for a convention to revise the treaties to make europe fit for the decades to come. you yourself have said change together. we have agreed on the ets, the social fund and the border adjustment text here. but i say, it's not enough. the viessmann example. the viessmann sale is a warning sign. our companies obviously don't believe in the green deal, the business case behind it. are we - this is the question - creating more jobs in china than in europe with our decisions? at least that has to be our driving force, and we have to see that competitiveness is back on the agenda. food production is also a big issue. consumer prices are rising, and food prices are part of the inflation. we should actually be exporting more food to north africa, which we are not, and at the same time our farmers are being styled as scapegoats. this too is not compatible with the need for a policy of cooperation. finally, mr chancellor, when you joined the spd, willy brandt made a promise. i quote: "dare to be more democratic". i would like to ask you: what does this mean for europe now? what does it mean for us in this parliament in 2024? for me, as a representative of the epp, an election means one candidate, one candidate and a programme, content, ideas, so that people can decide. it applies to the election of a mayor, a chancellor in germany, but also to the election of a president, a president of the european commission. unfortunately, the proposals of this parliament on the process of the top candidates and also on the transnational lists, which were decided by majority vote here, have not been adopted by either macron or merkel - i say this explicitly - or sánchez or you. i therefore ask you, in the spirit of willy brandt, to reduce the number of backrooms in the council and to increase democracy in this house, in the european parliament. let's start with the future. not tomorrow, but today.

the german chancellor. - madam president, ladies and gentlemen, i have to say that i am very pleased with the way in which the commission has dealt with this matter. i am very grateful for this debate and for the many contributions. first of all, i would like to address iratxe. although technology was not on our side, my attempts to learn spanish in spain were enough to understand well-spoken speech. thank you for that. we have great challenges ahead of us. we must renew, modernise and move the european union forward. this is our future together. we need a geopolitical union - i said that and i also spoke about the principles that should guide us. i would therefore like to make a very specific comment on some of the points raised here. if we are in favour of a large europe, if we are in favour of enlargement, then at the very least this must be a reason for us to carry out the necessary reforms to keep europe functioning. and that is why - no matter how you have always stood on this - when you are faced with the question: how do we come to the decision that enlargement can only be successful if we also have qualified majorities in the decision-making processes of the european union, at least in the central questions. and that is even possible without amending the contract, if we agree to it by consensus. this is not the nomination of minorities by any majorities, but this is the way we become able to act together in the constant search for compromise - what is necessary and what i believe is a great feature of successful democracy. i think that the commission has a duty to make a statement on this matter, and i think that the commission has a duty to make a statement on this matter. and of course we will have to discuss many other issues if we succeed in adding more members, because of course this will also mean changes for all the members who are already in the european union today, because we will then have to extend solidarity to more countries. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. and i want to say this explicitly and emphatically: it is really embarrassing that 20 years ago we opened up the possibility for the western balkan countries to become members of the european union, and that today we are still not there. the credibility of the european union depends on our success in this. that is why all the political families represented here should help, so that where we have made concrete agreements that make the accession process of all six western balkan states possible, we can also influence our own party family on the ground, for example, to agree to the necessary constitutional changes. that is my wish for the european people's party in the direction of north macedonia. it would be our common european concern that this should be possible. but we must work together to ensure that this is done in the knowledge that there can be no bilateralisation of accession processes - and we stand by that. this can be said very clearly to the citizens of the country. i want to work to bring about this reform and to do so in an orderly, common process. i believe that europe will only succeed if north, south, west and east understand each other as one, if we accept our differences but join forces to create a common force. i would like to repeat what i said in my speech: the world we live in soon, by the middle of this century, will have 10 billion people, and we'll still be just 450 to 500 million. if we do not come together, if we do not act together, then we will be irrelevant and we will not have a say in the future of the world. we want to get away from the old dominance of europe in previous centuries. but we want to be a good, fair partner in the world of the future, and for that we need our unity. it has been said rightly - that is what i want to say about the terrible russian attack on ukraine. and i want to make this very clear: this is a threat to peace and security in europe and the whole world. the fact is that the russian attack has heralded a turning point in the understanding of security in europe, the result of the policy of détente pursued by willy brandt and helmut schmidt and many others, namely that borders should no longer be pushed back by force. this is the understanding that was behind the csce and the osce and all the political processes. that was also the understanding in the nato-russia file. so russia has agreed on several occasions that this is the right way. and with the attack on ukraine, it's the imperialist vision that if you're stronger, you could just take a piece of the territory of the neighboring country. we cannot and will not accept that. and that is why we will support ukraine for as long as it needs to and can rely on it. i would like to say explicitly that i have listened with interest to one or another contribution from all political spectrums, and i have been happy to speak in german, but that has not always helped the matter. because one thing is quite clear, and this is perhaps the first thing to say in order to get the right perspective: germany is the biggest supporter of ukraine among the member states of the european union. we will remain so, and we are so because we want to support ukraine in its struggle for freedom and independence. germany is also a leader in the supply of arms. some people here have forgotten that because they are too far away, but that is the reality. and it will remain so. and when we decided to supply combat tanks in close cooperation and coordination with many others, for example in the field of tanks, many of those who had previously taken an active part in the debate were no longer to be found. this is the real reality. but it speaks for exactly what the majority of german citizens finds right: a cautious policy, always coordinated with allies and friends, no unilateral measures, but a decisive, balanced, responsible decision-making practice. this is what ukraine needs, and this is what germany is doing. i want to speak about energy policy, which has been discussed from various perspectives here, and i want to leave no doubt about the position of germany on this matter, which is unanimous and united in the government i lead. we are responsible for almost 2% - some people calculate a little differently - of the world's emissions, as has been pointed out from the right. and the question that some people ask is, why are we doing anything about climate change at all? my answer is: firstly, we are less than 2% of the world's population in relation to germany. and secondly, it's our economic strength, it's our engineers that we have in germany and europe, it's our companies and the technologies that we can develop that will help us to stop man-made climate change. i have to say this clearly. the citizens of the countries of south america, asia and africa are entitled to a standard of living and prosperity similar to those we have achieved in europe. and if we don't want to deny them that, then the only way that works for them and for us is if there are technologies that are as cheap as today's fossil-based industrial processes that make it possible to achieve that prosperity. and that's exactly what we do in germany. we've made a commitment to be carbon neutral by 2045. that means electrification and more electricity production, as was also mentioned here, that means hydrogen instead of coal, gas and oil, and that means from now until 2045 not 25 years. we must do this by investing now. and if germany, a country that because of its industrial strength, for example, and other countries are similarly producing and consuming 600 terawatt hours of electricity a year, can do that, by the end of this decade it will be a third more, and in the 1930s it will be 1,000 terawatt hours that we have to produce, all from renewable sources: wind from offshore wind farms, from onshore wind farms, from solar, from biomass and all the possibilities. and of course, with 24-hour electricity, which means that there has to be storage, that we need hydrogen-powered plants to deliver in the off-peak hours. all these decisions have to be made now. let us work together on this. because if we are honest, the parliament, the european commission, the german government and many others, the laws and regulations that we have built and crafted over the last decades and made with great love are not able to approve all these investments in time. many want to invest, the technologies are there. now we must have the courage to change the rules to help make this pace work. and for germany alone, that's five to six wind turbines a day, that's 40 football fields of solar panels a day, that's many 1,000 kilometers of power lines that need to be laid, that's a lot of offshore wind farms that all need to be approved piece by piece, much more than today. and that is why we must have the courage, as a commission, as a parliament, as a national government, to change laws so that we do not look at them all and say: what we have set out to do cannot succeed. but if we do that, we will have a great mobilization of innovation, we will have economic growth like europe has not had for a long time, and that will be good for the environment, but also good for jobs and prosperity in the future. i would like to say something about the question of common economic and financial policy. i think i have a good history to talk about, because the reconstruction fund, what we did during the corona crisis, that's what i tried to push and outline very substantially as germany's finance minister. and it was something very effective, because it was the message against a crisis that comes from such a threat as the pandemic all over germany and elsewhere: let's respond together with a solidarity action! and that we did that, it worked before the first funds were flowing, it strengthened the stability of the finances of all the member states so much that they could act and nobody had to leave out something that was important at that time. that's why everyone can rely on it: the german government will make all the necessary measures to show solidarity in such a crisis possible. but we also stand for stability in europe, including fiscal stability. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. the consequence is that we do not want to plunge any member state into an austerity crisis. but that does not mean that we think that everything is possible in fiscal terms, that stability is not a concern and that it is not important to limit the debt. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council. that will not work in the long run, and that is why it is important to strike the right balance on these issues. in this spirit, we shall succeed in formulating a common solution, even though there is still hard work ahead of us. but it is a contribution to solidarity that we must make. we have also been talking about the policy of détente, and i have already quoted willy brandt and helmut schmidt. but, mr. schirdewan, i'll tell you this: if you're moving through history, you have to stay accurate. the basis of the policy of détente pursued by willy brandt and helmut schmidt has always been a strong defence. we spent almost 4% of our economic output on defence at the time, because of the east-west conflict and the threat situation at the time. but if we say that in germany, like many others in europe, we want to spend 2% of our economic output to achieve the nato objectives, then this is exactly in line with the policy of the willy brandt and helmut schmidt tradition and is also the right response to a threat. because let's not forget and i want to say that again. let 's not forget: the russian attack is a threat because it says that the force of power is ready to override the law. and we cannot let that message go unheard. we can't pretend that's not the case. there is a clear message attached to the attack on ukraine. that's why it's our common task in nato to make sure that we make it clear to everyone that we are strong enough to defend ourselves, as a peaceful alliance that does not engage in aggression against others, that is a basis for us to return to that consensus in europe that should also exist worldwide: no revisionism, no violent shifting of borders. this is the basis of peace in the whole world. i am therefore looking forward to the partnership with the european parliament, to our future orientation and to the courage to move quickly. yes, there are sometimes situations in politics where you have to negotiate a lot, but you still have to get results. and yes, in a pluralistic world, in liberal democracies, in alliances of states, it is always the case that you have to come together. what we have in common is not something that comes over us like the holy spirit and then suddenly we all agree. this is achieved through democratic debate, by being aware and willing to come together with others in coalitions, among national communities, in parliaments, here too. and i ask that you all make the effort. the democratic debate, the debate, must lead to a result, and a good result for our common european future. (parliament adopted the resolution)
thank you very much, chancellor scholz, thank you dear colleagues. the debate is closed. written statements (rule 171)
in writing. i share your vision of an enlarged and reformed european union, and a first step in this direction can be taken quickly by resolving in the council the unjustified deadlock on romania and bulgaria's accession to the schengen area. you have underlined the spirit of european solidarity expressed by robert schuman 73 years ago, which is based on facts and concrete actions, but for romanian citizens this spirit is extinguished every time they cross borders, although the european treaties guarantee their right to free movement, while romania has been meeting the criteria for accession to the schengen area for over 11 years. i therefore ask you, as your leader in the european council, to exert the necessary pressure on the minority of dubious opponents to immediately correct this discriminatory situation, which risks turning romanian citizens from the most pro-european just a few years ago to the most eurosceptic in the near future.
rapporteur. – madam president, dear colleagues, i know how do you feel when someone is taking the floor in the middle of the votes. this is why i prepared a very short 45—minute speech. when i started working on urban mobility, i was thinking that it is all about trains, trams, buses, cars, bikes and, yes, about e—scooters. it turns out to be all about rights. the right of every person with disability in europe to use urban mobility networks. every mother with a baby carriage to use a good infrastructure prepared for her. it is all about those kids who are willing to ride a bike on a safe bike lane far from cars, trucks and buses. so if i have to say, yes, it is all about those who are willing to drive car as well. we should not treat them as criminals. let us not forget that those who are living in other most rural areas have no alternative. they have to move somehow and the car is their only solution. if i have to wrap up, there is a reason that the word ‘balance’ has a divine region. it is exactly in the middle between material and spiritual. in our days this means: yes, let’s adopt and encourage people to use new technologies, new modes of transport, but not turn our backs to modes of transport who serve us faithfully for decades.
rapporteur. – madam president, sorry colleagues, i don’t know how you feel, so i test your patience. european security has never been more fragile since the end of the second world war. russia’s full-scale war against ukraine is a turning point in the history of our continent. there is no other way for europe than to be prepared, both mentally and physically, to defend its people, economy, our way of life. this requires more adequate funding for defence. in the light of the ongoing war, we have primarily and rightly focused on the heavy iron and ammunition that ukraine needs to win this war. but there is also another dimension where the eu must deliver: new and critical technologies that are needed for our security and defence. the report we will be voting shortly comes as a reply to the commission’s roadmap of critical technologies for security and defence. with this report, we stress the need for europe to become more competitive in the field of new and disruptive technologies and to combine the best knowledge and practices of private sector, academia and government. we also emphasise the importance of close cooperation with like-minded partners globally. smes and startups have an important role to play in this innovation. more and better investment tools are needed for the innovation to take place. finally, we cannot afford more defence with less money. today, 1% of the mff dedicated for security and defence is not enough. dear colleagues, i kindly ask you to support this report with your vote.
the rapporteur. madam president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, the mediterranean is a european sea for half its length. europe cannot remain passive any longer in the face of the continuing regressions in this ecologically fragile area. these declines are further exacerbated by the warming climate to which the mediterranean is particularly vulnerable. climate scientists estimate that its impact will be 20% stronger than elsewhere in europe. this will quickly add to the problems facing the european citizens living there: fires, devastating storms, dwindling water resources, loss of biodiversity. on the other hand, human activities have an increasing impact on the sea, for example the increase in international maritime traffic passing through the sea, bringing air pollution and discharges to the sea, and also the increase in coastal populations, such as tourist attendance. no efficiency will be possible without the involvement of european added value. no one can claim to be able to provide solutions to these problems on their own, which must be tackled collectively. europe has many assets which give it a major responsibility in managing this area. it has the technical expertise of multiple actors. it has the administrative competence to steer a cooperation already foreshadowed by various programmes. it has the experience of macro-regional strategies already developed elsewhere, and it has, in addition to its technical and administrative capacity, an incomparably stronger financial capacity than that of other countries, both in the east and in the south. if the european union is committed to its responsibility for the northern part of the mediterranean basin, it will be a driving force for the countries and regions of the south and east. if it remains passive any longer, the situation will continue to deteriorate and europe will then bear the responsibility for not having exercised its capacity to act. this is the message that the european parliament is sending today with this report, which calls for the establishment of a macro-regional strategy for the mediterranean. in conclusion, i would like to thank the fictitious rapporteurs who have all contributed to this report.
rapporteur. – madam president, i understand it is the last vote, so i try to be as brief as possible. i stand before you as the rapporteur for the eu standardisation strategy in the imco committee to present my report on standardisation, a critical component in achieving the goals of our internal market. i am pleased to observe that standardisation is increasingly at the core of the eu’s digital and industrial strategy, acknowledging its strategic importance in shaping our future. my report aims to shed light on the strengths and weaknesses of the eu standardisation system and on recommendations to enhance its effectiveness. one of the key issues highlighted in the report is the need to clarify the division of responsibilities between the european commission and the standardisation organisations. another critical aspect is the necessity for international cooperation in standardisation. as global trade dynamics shift and become more complex, we must advocate for inclusive, like-minded approaches to standardisation at the international level. our commitment to a market-driven and voluntary process behind the standards is crucial in promoting a balanced global system. to conclude, i call upon all members of the european parliament to join me in promoting a more robust, forward-looking and inclusive standardisation strategy for the eu.
that concludes the vote. (the sitting was suspended for a few moments)

the rapporteur. the commission has already made a number of proposals. first of all, i should like to thank mr jeroen lenaers, who will speak after me, but, madam president, since you have just announced it at the time of the vote, i cannot know that we will start before then. i would also like to thank my shadow rapporteurs, with whom we have had a very good cooperation. i would like to thank the european court of auditors and its members, mr tony murphy and mr jan gregor, who are both here. i would like to thank you very much for the annual report and the special report and for the excellent cooperation. i would like to thank the commission, commissioner, and you too. at the beginning, cooperation was a bit bumpy in a few dgs, but i thank dg budg for its support in promoting cooperation. the greatest successes in relief that we have been able to achieve, i believe, lie in four areas. firstly, we want a digitisation drive, and the commission has promised to support it. we urgently need to improve interoperability, not only for the sake of transparency and traceability, but also to further reduce red tape, speed up processes and get information faster than it has been possible to do so so far. so we don't just need arachne, because it's a huge tool, but it doesn't add much value; we need real interoperable systems. secondly, the methodology for partial payments was one of the key requirements in the arf. first there was none, then there was one, and then there is now a quantification. i appreciate the speed of the work. i am also grateful that there is now an interactive platform with information on projects that have already been implemented or are in the process of being implemented, and also information on the final beneficiaries, which will obviously be available to everyone. we are now receiving information on the 100 largest recipients not just in a sub-area but for the entire arf per member state and we will receive it published accordingly. these have been wonderful achievements indeed for us. but there are still some problems. first, we still have no information on how much money actually entered the real economy. i've been asking around in spain. i was promised that we would get the data; i still haven't gotten any. that means we still have no information about: how much money did the project developers or those who make projects actually receive, and how much money did actually get to the people? secondly, we have to realize that the arf is starting off rather slowly. it was originally intended as a quick emergency instrument, but in reality implementation is being delayed. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. if we really want to boost the economy, boost jobs, boost recovery, then the consequence is that the projects must be realised, and they must also have real added value and function. thirdly, we are currently seeing projects originally transferred from cohesion to the arf, the corona reconstruction fund. the corona reconstruction fund is now putting projects back into cohesion. so we have a lively shift station of different projects. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. i have the impression that, in the implementation of the programme, the ambition and the outward appearance of some of them were perhaps greater than the actual reality. so there's a lot to do. but i would like to thank all those involved for the good cooperation.
rapporteur. – madam president, i always believe in ladies first, so i gave monica the opportunity to speak before me. now, thank you very much, and i think, really, this discharge procedure is one of the most important procedures we do in the house because in order to maintain citizens trust in the work we do, we need to make sure that the budget is spent correctly. this house has a great responsibility to scrutinise that spending. i would really like to thank the co-rapporteur, the shadow rapporteurs, the secretariat and all colleagues who took part in this work; we had an excellent cooperation. i would also like to express my gratitude to the work of the european court of auditors, which is an indispensable partner in the work that we do. this discharge focuses on the year 2021, and we have observed a concerning increase in the error rate for expenditure, which now stands at 3 %, as reported by the court of auditors. this is well above the acceptable limit of 2 %. in fact, for the majority of reimbursement-based expenditures, the error rate reaches an alarming 4.7 % and we must take immediate action to prevent this rate from growing even further in the upcoming years. and we are urging the commission to launch additional initiatives to address this. the different methodologies used by the commission and the court of auditors when calculating the risk at payment or the error rate also complicates the comparability, it makes it difficult to compare the two numbers. this further adds confusion because the commission, whenever the court of auditors presents an error rate which is positive, we use this in our communications, we accept it, whenever there is an error rate presented which is unfavourable to the commission, the methodology is disputed. this is an approach which is not fair and we should also change that in order to make it all a bit more logical. even more alarming in that sense is the lack of responsibility and the failure to fully recognise shortcomings in the methodology with regards to climate spending. the court calculated that spending on climate action has been overstated by eur 72 billion for the 2014–2020 period, and the commission refuses to fully acknowledge it – commissioner timmermans came to a cont meeting and concluded by saying that we should agree to disagree on this. sorry, but no, we cannot agree to disagree on overstating eur 72 billion of climate spending. we need to accept the conclusions of the court on this and work to address this issue. another concern that we are addressing involves the slow absorption of funds and the rate of commitment to implementation, which could lead to increased risk towards the end of the budgetary period and to resolve these issues, we need to continue to bolster the administrative capacity of the member states and the european commission. now, we have in this discharge, really emphasised the significance of the union budget’s performance, which includes utilising appropriate indicators, simplifying rules and procedures and implementing better-targeted controls. transparency is a key element of a resolution and we emphasise the importance of transparent operations across the board, including for ngo and intermediaries in terms of their funding and ownership. and we also request that the commission establish an effective mechanism to ensure that ngo activities align with eu values. we have also underscored the importance of incorporating new technologies like edes and arachne and developing a unified information and monitoring system provided by the commission to allow for the electronic recording and storage of data of union funding recipients, including their beneficial ownerships, in order to help us prevent any misuse of the budget. we also recognise the benefits and lessons learned through the use of new technologies, such as satellite controls and agricultural spending, for instance. we are convinced that these advancements will play a crucial role in better safeguarding our budget. now, to wrap up on a very serious note, we remain deeply concerned – and this is also reflected in the resolution – about the rule of law situation in several member states, which not only undermines democracy but also results in substantial risks for the union budget. we expect all member states to adhere to democracy and the rule of law in order to receive european funds. and we particularly highlight the concerning situation in hungary, where corruption and an opaque public procurement system must be addressed through reforms within the context of the conditionality mechanism. i am grateful for the level of unity we achieved during our work in the committee, and i look forward to listening to all the contributions of our colleagues.
i am sorry to have to announce a quick suspension in the chamber because there is a technical problem linked to a difficulty in translating some languages, so i ask you to be patient and to stall for a few minutes just to solve this technical problem. (the sitting was suspended at 1.19 p.m. for technical reasons)
