a uachtaráin, across europe, young people struggled to secure a roof over their heads and to meet the rising costs of living. ireland, once known for one of the highest eu homeownership rates, has seen a decline despite near full employment and recent economic growth. notably, this decline is prominent among younger and lower-income demographics, with less than a third of 30-year-olds owning homes today. this, of course, is a problem yet recent actions have been hampered by the pandemic and having to virtually shut down the construction sector, as well as mortgage interest rates and building costs shooting up. despite lofty promises from politicians seeking support, unfortunately there is no quick fix. the solution in some ways is straightforward: increasing the supply of affordable housing. however, achieving this requires various policy measures at different governance levels. streamlining and simplifying planning systems at a national level is essential, but authorities must have adequate resources for this to occur. housing is not an eu competence. however, the eu can and should strengthen its financial contribution by building upon the effective work of the european investment bank. ireland itself needs to be better at utilising existing eu funds. for example, the construction of social and affordable housing could be covered under investeu. although it is welcome that the commission approved a eur 450 million irish housing scheme under state aid rules, there also needs to be broadening of exemptions for social housing under the rulebook more generally.
mr. chairman, i think we can all agree that crises affect young people in a specific way. some important life steps we've all taken in our lives have been taken away from them. we say home is a feeling, not a place. however, the young will tell you and the place is. even before the crisis, as many as 30 percent of 15- to 29-year-olds were in overcrowded housing, and most cannot dream of owning their own property. rents and rentals exploded, and the price of a square of housing was even higher. in most member states, this increase was not even remotely observed. in my country croatia, more than 55% of people have trouble paying their rent, which is almost 20% higher than the eu average. on the other hand, young people in croatia stay in a home for much longer than the european union average, up to age 33, almost the longest in the european union, behind portugal. this is certainly contributed by a 20% increase in rents and a 30% increase in real estate. however, we are not just talking about an economic problem. it's also about mental health issues. even 60% of citizens in the european union say that living conditions are the main cause. the consequences of these problems are unmistakable. that's why we socialists are looking for a european answer to this problem. we need new regulations on the housing market. we need to stop the price inflation. we need to end inequalities in society.

mr president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, after the pilot phase, the short-term instruments and the most sophisticated but not adequately financed instruments, we have now reached the real testbed for our ambitions to create a defence europe. we set high ambition levels but we are prepared to support them adequately at all stages of this journey. it will be essential to report on the various initiatives previously launched under the edip, thus providing the financial resources necessary for the various programmes to be truly effective, starting with the european defence fund. if we really want to strengthen our industrial capacity, we must ensure that financing is predictable and continuous, not linked to short-term and short-term 'spot' initiatives. it will also be essential to provide for common procurement mechanisms for products developed under the european defence fund, thus separating us from the logic of marginal incentives and supporting the entire production cycle of defence equipment. the content we give and the funds we allocate to edip will tell us who and what we want to become; the future is in our hands, but we must act wisely. this is not the time for ambiguity and hesitation: we must say yes to a strong and equitable europe of defence and prepare ourselves to support it adequately so that our ambition is in perfect proportion to our strengths.
the commission has already decided to take a decision on the proposal for a directive on the approximation of the laws of the member states relating to the transport of dangerous goods. for almost two years, the war criminal in the kremlin has been waging a full-scale war in the middle of europe. if we don't give full support to ukraine, it will march through and then it won't stop. we must react, and we should have done so from the outset, both at national level where the defence budget is, and with our european programmes that complement it. we did a part with asap and edirpa, yes, but we still don't see the results of that. that's why it's so important that there are follow-up programs. but now, in view of the dramatic situation in the european council under the multiannual financial framework, it would be necessary to decide that edip would actually be financed and that we would be making at least 5 billion extra this weekend. but unfortunately, that's not going to happen. and why? because the seriousness of the situation is not recognized from my point of view. i would like to say that the commissioner is not a member of the commission. i believe that the original plan was to put an edis before edip, which was to present the strategy first and then to orient the programme after the strategy. that could have been the case. i am not accusing the commission of not having the money. the blame is more likely to be directed at member states that are unable to make the funds available in an enlarged financial framework in such a way that a credible programme can actually be produced. the war criminal runs three shifts, seven days a week full war economy, and we're trying to get the production up and running here with the smallest programs. we must recognize the seriousness of the situation. we must not only implement this programme here, we must increase the capacity of all national defence budgets so that we can counter the aggressor.
madam president, dear commissioner, what do we need to make edip relevant? we need money, political will and credible implementation. money alone won’t do. without the political will and a shared platform for cooperation, it will be a waste – it will further deepen the gaps and lack of interoperability. so money needs to be invested in a way that brings forward our political objectives, which in turn must come from a shared and fully assumed sense of necessity and urgency about our defence in europe. yes, defence is a national competence. yes, it is outside the treaty. yes, we have nato. yes, we have an uneven spread of industrial capabilities across the union. but all that will never change if we are not prepared to make bold decisions now. it is time to recognise that we have to invest to become the owners of our own defence. and for that we need to work towards a fully-fledged defence union, and that path must be prepared by an instrument such as edip and a designated commissioner for defence in the future.
mrs president, ladies and gentlemen, we have lived in peace in europe for many years, for decades, and fortunately we are accustomed to the concept of peace. then, on the brink of the fall of the berlin wall, we had to recognize a new concept that is risk, and then, because of terrorism, we had to come to the concept of threat. then what happened in february 2022 happened, so history went back 70 years and concepts like "war" and "enemy" came back to life. the european union's reaction has been exceptional, more has been done in a few weeks than in more than 20 years. but as you rightly said, commissioner, that was a reaction; the commission has already decided to adopt a number of measures to combat the spread of the virus. we therefore expect the edip to be a real message of hope, as well as a clear message from the point of view of financial resources, in order to aspire to a future europe where the subject of defence is no longer a subject of the states but of the european union, and neither from the financial point of view a subject of the states but of the european union. today we spend a lot, about 250 billion euros a year on defence, but we have 17 different types of cartridge. in the united states, they spend three times as much on a single tank type. the hope is that the europe we have seen in the defense of ukraine will be the europe of the future, united and free from envy between the states.
madam president, mr navarro ríos, mr commissioner breton, ladies and gentlemen, the events of recent years around the world and in our immediate neighbourhood have made it clear that the european union needs a very serious investment programme in the defence industry, but also a common strategy and an army that can ensure our security, the union. this programme must take into account the need for all equipment, ammunition to be produced here in the european union, but also for industry to be able to adapt to the needs of different times. this program must pay attention not only to classical weapons but also to the needs of new technologies, even new types of warfare. we're not just talking about a classic war, as we know from history books, one that uses the latest technologies, but also the information war that third countries have already started. we can't always rely on others to produce the ammunition we need, to provide the defense we need, to counter the information attacks we face. we need to start relying on ourselves.
on behalf of the s&d group, mr president, mr commissioner, mr president-in-office of the council, i am again referring to frontex, the european border and coast guard agency, in a debate with a resolution in parliament's plenary session: a resolution resulting from the frontex control working party we have set up in the committee on civil liberties, justice and home affairs, which has been responsible for strengthening the mandate of frontex to become the giant agency of the european constellation, with a budget that has grown from 114 million to 800 million a year and 10 000 uniform officers with the right to bear arms. and we will not stop, in this exercise of control over frontex, until we ensure that it fulfils its mandate to respect fundamental rights by deploying its 40 officers under a head of the fundamental rights agency, also in cooperation with third countries and also in search and rescue operations. and we will not stop until there is no suspicion of mismanagement of european funds, such as the one that led to the olaf investigations and the resignation of its former director. and we will not stop until we have no credibility in the reports that continue to come to us from frontex's involvement in pushbacks, illegal returns, let alone cooperation with militias that return people apprehended at sea to unsafe countries. this is the commitment of this european parliament to the european border and coast guard agency.
on behalf of the renew group. – mr president, commissioner, dear minister, dear colleagues, our union is as strong as its institutions and these institutions, bodies and agencies are only as strong as their credibility and the manner in which they fulfil the mandate received from all citizens. credibility is linked with transparency, and at times it requires the courage to admit when mistakes are made, to draw the right lessons from those mistakes, and to communicate both the good and the bad, the achievements, but also the shortcomings. this is how we gain the trust of our citizens, by showing them that we are constantly trying to improve and deliver to the best of our ability on their needs. with frontex, we had arrived at such an inflection point two years ago when the credibility of its work, otherwise immensely important for our union and done with dedication and professionalism on multiple occasions, was put to the test by bad management and the naive belief that ugly things can be kept under the carpet. well, they cannot. and it took ombudsman and olaf investigations to flesh them out. on our part, as parliament, we organised ourselves in the scrutiny group and together across political groups we delivered a strong message as to what the expectations for change were. and now, with this resolution, we put a final touch on those findings and we draw on what failed and had to be corrected: things like reporting mechanisms, role of the frontex fundamental rights officer, the interplay between frontex and national border authorities. but we also highlight the progress made. since agency changed at the very top, the advances in new procedures, better accountability and oversight with visible effects in operational terms, in important pressure points at our borders such as moldova or ukraine.
